I had all my social work textbooks in there, and this beautiful spider plant that I’ve kept alive since grad school. I had to move the plant to the kitchen, and it’s just not getting the same light there, you know? The leaves are turning a bit yellow at the tips, which reminds me I need to buy some of that liquid fertilizer. The hardware store down the street sells it, but the owner is always closing early on Tuesdays for some reason. I mean, I take a daily multivitamin, but I've been taking the same brand for years. I always buy it at the independent pharmacy on the corner, the one with the vintage neon sign in the window? It buzzes a little bit when it's raining outside. The pharmacist there, Brenda, she always wears these incredible hand-knit cardigans. She told me she actually sources the yarn herself from a women's cooperative in Peru, which I just think is so wonderful. Supporting fair trade is so important... in social work, you really see the impact of economic disparities firsthand, so I try to be mindful of where I shop. No, no, nothing like that. I've never had any thyroid issues or anything neurological. I mean, my aunt had some thyroid trouble a few years back, and she had to completely change her diet. She started eating all these strange seaweeds and things, which she bought from this tiny little health food store near her house. The owner of that store actually used to be a jazz musician, if you can believe it. He played the saxophone, and he'd sometimes just play right there in the aisles while you were browsing the vitamins. It was quite an experience, really. I always thought it would be wonderful to learn an instrument, maybe the cello? It just has such a mournful, beautiful sound. I saw a cellist playing in the subway once, right at the 34th Street station, and the acoustics down there just made the notes linger in the air... but no, my own health has been fine. No strokes or Parkinson's or anything like that. Just the mood changes I was telling you about.

Sleep? Well, I haven't really noticed any big changes with my sleep. I mean, I go to bed around the same time, usually after I finish my chamomile tea. Sometimes I'll read a little bit before turning off the light. Right now I'm reading this fascinating biography of Eleanor Roosevelt. She was just such an incredible advocate, you know? The way she used her position to champion human rights... it really makes you think about the impact one person can have. It reminds me of why I got into social work in the first place. I actually met a woman at a conference once who had met Eleanor when she was a little girl. She said she had the warmest smile. The conference was in Chicago, right near the lake, and the wind coming off that water in November is just piercing. I had to buy a completely new scarf while I was there, this lovely thick wool one, plum colored. I still wear it when it gets really cold here. But um, yeah, my sleep has been pretty normal, I think. Foggy? No, I don't think so. My mind feels pretty clear, honestly. I actually do the crossword puzzle every morning while I have my tea, just to keep everything sharp. She told me she actually sources the yarn herself from a women's cooperative in Peru, which I just think is so wonderful. Supporting fair trade is so important... in social work, you really see the impact of economic disparities firsthand, so I try to be mindful of where I shop. I’ve always wanted to visit Peru, actually, maybe hike the Inca Trail, but I read that the altitude sickness can be absolutely brutal. \n\nBut, um... no, I haven't been physically ill myself. And I don't use any substances or anything. I mostly just drink chamomile tea in the evenings. I have this beautiful ceramic mug with little blue flowers painted on it that I use every night... but yeah, no new medicines or sickness.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any medical or neurological condition, such as thyroid disease, Parkinson’s disease, stroke, or multiple sclerosis?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I've never had any thyroid issues or anything neurological. I mean, my aunt had some thyroid trouble a few years back, and she had to completely change her diet. She started eating all these strange seaweeds and things, which she bought from this tiny little health food store near her house. The owner of that store actually used to be a jazz musician, if you can believe it. He played the saxophone, and he'd sometimes just play right there in the aisles while you were browsing the vitamins. It was quite an experience, really. I always thought it would be wonderful to learn an instrument, maybe the cello? It just has such a mournful, beautiful sound. I saw a cellist playing in the subway once, right at the 34th Street station, and the acoustics down there just made the notes linger in the air... but no, my own health has been fine. No strokes or Parkinson's or anything like that. Just the mood changes I was telling you about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more, sleeping less, or having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Well, I haven't really noticed any big changes with my sleep. I mean, I go to bed around the same time, usually after I finish my chamomile tea. Sometimes I'll read a little bit before turning off the light. Right now I'm reading this fascinating biography of Eleanor Roosevelt. She was just such an incredible advocate, you know? The way she used her position to champion human rights... it really makes you think about the impact one person can have. It reminds me of why I got into social work in the first place. I actually met a woman at a conference once who had met Eleanor when she was a little girl. She said she had the warmest smile. The conference was in Chicago, right near the lake, and the wind coming off that water in November is just piercing. I had to buy a completely new scarf while I was there, this lovely thick wool one, plum colored. I still wear it when it gets really cold here. But um, yeah, my sleep has been pretty normal, I think. I haven't been sleeping more or less than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had periods where your thinking felt noticeably slower or more foggy than usual?\n\nPatient: Foggy? No, I don't think so. My mind feels pretty clear, honestly. I actually do the crossword puzzle every morning while I have my tea, just to keep everything sharp. I use a fountain pen for it, which my coworker David says is incredibly arrogant of me. He has this very dry sense of humor... he actually used to do stand-up comedy at these little clubs down in the Village back in the nineties. He brought in a VHS tape of one of his old sets once to show us in the breakroom. It's so funny to see those old tapes, you know? It really takes you back. I remember if the VCR ate the ribbon, you had to sit there and carefully wind it all back in with a pencil. I still keep a bunch of pencils on my desk at the clinic in this little ceramic cup shaped like an owl. My niece made it for me when she was in middle school. She's just so talented with her hands, she's actually studying architecture now up in Boston. Confusion? No, I always know exactly where I am. I've got a pretty good sense of direction, actually. Even when I first moved to the city, I never really got lost. I used to just walk for hours on the weekends, exploring all the different neighborhoods. I found this amazing little bakery down in Little Italy once—just the most incredible cannolis you've ever had. The pastry cream was so light, not heavy and cloying like some places make it. The woman who ran it was originally from Naples, and she told me all about how her grandmother taught her the recipe. She had these beautiful black and white photographs of her family on the wall behind the counter. One of them was of her grandfather standing next to this classic vintage car... I think it was an Alfa Romeo? I've always loved those old cars, the curves on them are just like art. My dad used to restore classic cars in our garage when I was growing up. He spent three years working on a '67 Mustang. He painted it this deep, cherry red. The smell of oil and paint always reminds me of him... but anyway, no, I haven't had any trouble knowing where I am or anything like that. I'm totally oriented.

And I don't use any substances or anything. I mostly just drink chamomile tea in the evenings. I have this beautiful ceramic mug with little blue flowers painted on it that I use every night... but yeah, no new medicines or sickness.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any medical or neurological condition, such as thyroid disease, Parkinson’s disease, stroke, or multiple sclerosis?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I've never had any thyroid issues or anything neurological. I mean, my aunt had some thyroid trouble a few years back, and she had to completely change her diet. She started eating all these strange seaweeds and things, which she bought from this tiny little health food store near her house. The owner of that store actually used to be a jazz musician, if you can believe it. He played the saxophone, and he'd sometimes just play right there in the aisles while you were browsing the vitamins. It was quite an experience, really. I always thought it would be wonderful to learn an instrument, maybe the cello? It just has such a mournful, beautiful sound. I saw a cellist playing in the subway once, right at the 34th Street station, and the acoustics down there just made the notes linger in the air... but no, my own health has been fine. No strokes or Parkinson's or anything like that. Just the mood changes I was telling you about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more, sleeping less, or having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Well, I haven't really noticed any big changes with my sleep. I mean, I go to bed around the same time, usually after I finish my chamomile tea. Sometimes I'll read a little bit before turning off the light. Right now I'm reading this fascinating biography of Eleanor Roosevelt. She was just such an incredible advocate, you know? The way she used her position to champion human rights... it really makes you think about the impact one person can have. It reminds me of why I got into social work in the first place. I actually met a woman at a conference once who had met Eleanor when she was a little girl. She said she had the warmest smile. The conference was in Chicago, right near the lake, and the wind coming off that water in November is just piercing. I had to buy a completely new scarf while I was there, this lovely thick wool one, plum colored. I still wear it when it gets really cold here. But um, yeah, my sleep has been pretty normal, I think. I haven't been sleeping more or less than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had periods where your thinking felt noticeably slower or more foggy than usual?\n\nPatient: Foggy? No, I don't think so. My mind feels pretty clear, honestly. I actually do the crossword puzzle every morning while I have my tea, just to keep everything sharp. I use a fountain pen for it, which my coworker David says is incredibly arrogant of me. He has this very dry sense of humor... he actually used to do stand-up comedy at these little clubs down in the Village back in the nineties. He brought in a VHS tape of one of his old sets once to show us in the breakroom. It's so funny to see those old tapes, you know? It really takes you back. I remember if the VCR ate the ribbon, you had to sit there and carefully wind it all back in with a pencil. I still keep a bunch of pencils on my desk at the clinic in this little ceramic cup shaped like an owl. My niece made it for me when she was in middle school. She's just so talented with her hands, she's actually studying architecture now up in Boston. The winters there are just brutal, though, much worse than here, with all that historic brick just freezing through. But... wait, I'm sorry, what were you asking? Oh, right, my thinking. No, no slowness or fog or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of confusion or trouble knowing where you are?\n\nPatient: Confusion? No, I always know exactly where I am. I've got a pretty good sense of direction, actually. Even when I first moved to the city, I never really got lost. I found this amazing little bakery down in Little Italy once—just the most incredible cannolis you've ever had. The pastry cream was so light, not heavy and cloying like some places make it. He spent three years working on a '67 Mustang. He painted it this deep, cherry red. The smell of oil and paint always reminds me of him... but anyway, no, I haven't had any trouble knowing where I am or anything like that. Sad? No, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad or down. I mean, everyone has their moments, but no, not most of the day or anything like that. I actually try to keep a pretty positive outlook. I used to go to this studio downtown, right above a bakery, and the smell of the sourdough would drift up during shavasana. It was wonderful. I even loved going to art galleries and journaling every evening. I have stacks of journals in my apartment, these beautiful leather-bound ones I found at a flea market years ago. The vendor was this lovely older man who told me stories about his travels in Morocco... but anyway, lately, I’m mostly just moving through obligations. Just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Has this loss of interest and pleasure been going on most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: Two years? Oh, no, not that long. It’s really been about eight months. That's when this whole change in how I’m thinking, working, and connecting with people really started. It was right around the time we took my older relative into our apartment. We had to clear out the spare room for her, which used to be my study. I had all my social work textbooks in there, and this beautiful spider plant that I’ve kept alive since grad school. I had to move the plant to the kitchen, and it’s just not getting the same light there, you know? The leaves are turning a bit yellow at the tips, which reminds me I need to buy some of that liquid fertilizer. The hardware store down the street sells it, but the owner is always closing early on Tuesdays for some reason. He says it's for inventory, but I always see him walking his golden retriever in the park on Tuesday afternoons. Beautiful dog, really... but yes, anyway, it hasn't been two years. Just about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Did these mood and interest changes start after you began a new medication, substance, or after a medical illness?\n\nPatient: No, no new medications or anything like that. I mean, I take a daily multivitamin, but I've been taking the same brand for years. I always buy it at the independent pharmacy on the corner, the one with the vintage neon sign in the window? It buzzes a little bit when it's raining outside. The pharmacist there, Brenda, she always wears these incredible hand-knit cardigans. She told me she actually sources the yarn herself from a women's cooperative in Peru, which I just think is so wonderful. Supporting fair trade is so important... in social work, you really see the impact of economic disparities firsthand, so I try to be mindful of where I shop. I’ve always wanted to visit Peru, actually, maybe hike the Inca Trail, but I read that the altitude sickness can be absolutely brutal. \n\nBut, um... no, I haven't been physically ill myself. And I don't use any substances or anything. I mostly just drink chamomile tea in the evenings. I have this beautiful ceramic mug with little blue flowers painted on it that I use every night... but yeah, no new medicines or sickness.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any medical or neurological condition, such as thyroid disease, Parkinson’s disease, stroke, or multiple sclerosis?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I've never had any thyroid issues or anything neurological. I mean, my aunt had some thyroid trouble a few years back, and she had to completely change her diet. She started eating all these strange seaweeds and things, which she bought from this tiny little health food store near her house. The owner of that store actually used to be a jazz musician, if you can believe it. He played the saxophone, and he'd sometimes just play right there in the aisles while you were browsing the vitamins. It was quite an experience, really. I always thought it would be wonderful to learn an instrument, maybe the cello? It just has such a mournful, beautiful sound. I saw a cellist playing in the subway once, right at the 34th Street station, and the acoustics down there just made the notes linger in the air... but no, my own health has been fine. No strokes or Parkinson's or anything like that. Just the mood changes I was telling you about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more, sleeping less, or having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Well, I haven't really noticed any big changes with my sleep. I mean, I go to bed around the same time, usually after I finish my chamomile tea. Sometimes I'll read a little bit before turning off the light. Right now I'm reading this fascinating biography of Eleanor Roosevelt. She was just such an incredible advocate, you know? The way she used her position to champion human rights... it really makes you think about the impact one person can have. It reminds me of why I got into social work in the first place. I actually met a woman at a conference once who had met Eleanor when she was a little girl. She said she had the warmest smile. The conference was in Chicago, right near the lake, and the wind coming off that water in November is just piercing. I had to buy a completely new scarf while I was there, this lovely thick wool one, plum colored. I still wear it when it gets really cold here. But um, yeah, my sleep has been pretty normal, I think. I haven't been sleeping more or less than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had periods where your thinking felt noticeably slower or more foggy than usual?\n\nPatient: Foggy? No, I don't think so. My mind feels pretty clear, honestly. I actually do the crossword puzzle every morning while I have my tea, just to keep everything sharp. I use a fountain pen for it, which my coworker David says is incredibly arrogant of me. He has this very dry sense of humor... he actually used to do stand-up comedy at these little clubs down in the Village back in the nineties. He brought in a VHS tape of one of his old sets once to show us in the breakroom. It's so funny to see those old tapes, you know? It really takes you back. I remember if the VCR ate the ribbon, you had to sit there and carefully wind it all back in with a pencil. I still keep a bunch of pencils on my desk at the clinic in this little ceramic cup shaped like an owl. My niece made it for me when she was in middle school. She's just so talented with her hands, she's actually studying architecture now up in Boston. The winters there are just brutal, though, much worse than here, with all that historic brick just freezing through. But... wait, I'm sorry, what were you asking? Oh, right, my thinking. No, no slowness or fog or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of confusion or trouble knowing where you are?\n\nPatient: Confusion? No, I always know exactly where I am. I've got a pretty good sense of direction, actually. Even when I first moved to the city, I never really got lost. I used to just walk for hours on the weekends, exploring all the different neighborhoods. I found this amazing little bakery down in Little Italy once—just the most incredible cannolis you've ever had. The pastry cream was so light, not heavy and cloying like some places make it. The woman who ran it was originally from Naples, and she told me all about how her grandmother taught her the recipe. She had these beautiful black and white photographs of her family on the wall behind the counter. One of them was of her grandfather standing next to this classic vintage car... I think it was an Alfa Romeo? I've always loved those old cars, the curves on them are just like art. My dad used to restore classic cars in our garage when I was growing up. He spent three years working on a '67 Mustang. He painted it this deep, cherry red. The smell of oil and paint always reminds me of him... but anyway, no, I haven't had any trouble knowing where I am or anything like that. I'm totally oriented.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad or down. I mean, everyone has their moments, but no, not most of the day or anything like that. I actually try to keep a pretty positive outlook. I have this little daily affirmations calendar on my desk at work that really helps set the tone for the morning. It's one of those page-a-day ones, with pictures of national parks. Today's was Yosemite. I went there once, years ago, on a camping trip with some friends from grad school. We pitched our tents right in the valley, and waking up to see El Capitan in the morning light... it was just breathtaking. Though, honestly, I'm not much of a camper anymore. Sleeping on the ground really does a number on your back once you get past forty. My friend Sarah, who went on that trip with us, she actually ended up becoming a park ranger out in Colorado. She sends me a postcard every Christmas with a different mountain peak on it. I have them all pinned up on a corkboard in my kitchen. The corkboard is framed in this really pretty reclaimed barn wood that I found at a flea market upstate... but um, yeah, I'm not feeling sad. My mood has been fine, really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

She sends me a postcard every Christmas with a different mountain peak on it. I have them all pinned up on a corkboard in my kitchen. The corkboard is framed in this really pretty reclaimed barn wood that I found at a flea market upstate... but um, yeah, I'm not feeling sad. My mood has been fine, really.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost your appetite?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / mental_health_or_substance_abuse_social_worker / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Appetite? No, not really. I mean, I'm still eating my normal meals. I usually make a turkey sandwich for lunch to take to the clinic. I like to use that thick-cut whole wheat bread, the kind with all the seeds in it? It's so much better than that flimsy white bread that just turns to mush the second you put a slice of tomato on it. I actually grew my own tomatoes last summer in these big terracotta pots on my balcony. They were those little cherry tomatoes, the Sun Gold ones, so sweet you could just eat them like candy. I had this one pot that just went crazy, vines spilling everywhere. I had to tie them up to the railing with some twine. I buy my twine from this little hardware store down the street, it's one of those great old places where the floors creak and they have every size of screw sorted into these tiny little wooden drawers. The guy who owns it, Mr. Henderson, he's been there since the seventies. He always gives my dog a treat when we walk by. He keeps a jar of milk bones right by the register... but yeah, I'm still eating fine. My appetite hasn't changed.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... what finally brought me in is that I feel like the part of me that used to reach out for people, or take pleasure in things, has just gone very quiet. I used to love my work, you know? I'm a social worker, and I've always felt this deep connection to helping others navigate their own struggles. It felt vital. And yoga, too. I used to go to this studio downtown, right above a bakery, and the smell of the sourdough would drift up during shavasana. It was wonderful. I even loved going to art galleries and journaling every evening. I have stacks of journals in my apartment, these beautiful leather-bound ones I found at a flea market years ago. The vendor was this lovely older man who told me stories about his travels in Morocco... but anyway, lately, I’m mostly just moving through obligations. Just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Has this loss of interest and pleasure been going on most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: Two years? Oh, no, not that long. It’s really been about eight months. That's when this whole change in how I’m thinking, working, and connecting with people really started. It was right around the time we took my older relative into our apartment. We had to clear out the spare room for her, which used to be my study. I had all my social work textbooks in there, and this beautiful spider plant that I’ve kept alive since grad school. I had to move the plant to the kitchen, and it’s just not getting the same light there, you know? The leaves are turning a bit yellow at the tips, which reminds me I need to buy some of that liquid fertilizer. The hardware store down the street sells it, but the owner is always closing early on Tuesdays for some reason. He says it's for inventory, but I always see him walking his golden retriever in the park on Tuesday afternoons. Beautiful dog, really... but yes, anyway, it hasn't been two years. Just about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Did these mood and interest changes start after you began a new medication, substance, or after a medical illness?\n\nPatient: No, no new medications or anything like that. I mean, I take a daily multivitamin, but I've been taking the same brand for years. I always buy it at the independent pharmacy on the corner, the one with the vintage neon sign in the window? It buzzes a little bit when it's raining outside. The pharmacist there, Brenda, she always wears these incredible hand-knit cardigans. She told me she actually sources the yarn herself from a women's cooperative in Peru, which I just think is so wonderful. Supporting fair trade is so important... in social work, you really see the impact of economic disparities firsthand, so I try to be mindful of where I shop. I’ve always wanted to visit Peru, actually, maybe hike the Inca Trail, but I read that the altitude sickness can be absolutely brutal. \n\nBut, um... no, I haven't been physically ill myself. And I don't use any substances or anything. I mostly just drink chamomile tea in the evenings. I have this beautiful ceramic mug with little blue flowers painted on it that I use every night... but yeah, no new medicines or sickness.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any medical or neurological condition, such as thyroid disease, Parkinson’s disease, stroke, or multiple sclerosis?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I've never had any thyroid issues or anything neurological. I mean, my aunt had some thyroid trouble a few years back, and she had to completely change her diet. She started eating all these strange seaweeds and things, which she bought from this tiny little health food store near her house. The owner of that store actually used to be a jazz musician, if you can believe it. He played the saxophone, and he'd sometimes just play right there in the aisles while you were browsing the vitamins. It was quite an experience, really. I always thought it would be wonderful to learn an instrument, maybe the cello? It just has such a mournful, beautiful sound. I saw a cellist playing in the subway once, right at the 34th Street station, and the acoustics down there just made the notes linger in the air... but no, my own health has been fine. No strokes or Parkinson's or anything like that. Just the mood changes I was telling you about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more, sleeping less, or having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Well, I haven't really noticed any big changes with my sleep. I mean, I go to bed around the same time, usually after I finish my chamomile tea. Sometimes I'll read a little bit before turning off the light. Right now I'm reading this fascinating biography of Eleanor Roosevelt. She was just such an incredible advocate, you know? The way she used her position to champion human rights... it really makes you think about the impact one person can have. It reminds me of why I got into social work in the first place. I actually met a woman at a conference once who had met Eleanor when she was a little girl. She said she had the warmest smile. The conference was in Chicago, right near the lake, and the wind coming off that water in November is just piercing. I had to buy a completely new scarf while I was there, this lovely thick wool one, plum colored. I still wear it when it gets really cold here. But um, yeah, my sleep has been pretty normal, I think. I haven't been sleeping more or less than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had periods where your thinking felt noticeably slower or more foggy than usual?\n\nPatient: Foggy? No, I don't think so. My mind feels pretty clear, honestly. I actually do the crossword puzzle every morning while I have my tea, just to keep everything sharp. I use a fountain pen for it, which my coworker David says is incredibly arrogant of me. He has this very dry sense of humor... he actually used to do stand-up comedy at these little clubs down in the Village back in the nineties. He brought in a VHS tape of one of his old sets once to show us in the breakroom. It's so funny to see those old tapes, you know? It really takes you back. I remember if the VCR ate the ribbon, you had to sit there and carefully wind it all back in with a pencil. I still keep a bunch of pencils on my desk at the clinic in this little ceramic cup shaped like an owl. My niece made it for me when she was in middle school. She's just so talented with her hands, she's actually studying architecture now up in Boston. The winters there are just brutal, though, much worse than here, with all that historic brick just freezing through. But... wait, I'm sorry, what were you asking? Oh, right, my thinking. No, no slowness or fog or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of confusion or trouble knowing where you are?\n\nPatient: Confusion? No, I always know exactly where I am. I've got a pretty good sense of direction, actually. Even when I first moved to the city, I never really got lost. I used to just walk for hours on the weekends, exploring all the different neighborhoods. I found this amazing little bakery down in Little Italy once—just the most incredible cannolis you've ever had. The pastry cream was so light, not heavy and cloying like some places make it. The woman who ran it was originally from Naples, and she told me all about how her grandmother taught her the recipe. She had these beautiful black and white photographs of her family on the wall behind the counter. One of them was of her grandfather standing next to this classic vintage car... I think it was an Alfa Romeo? I've always loved those old cars, the curves on them are just like art. My dad used to restore classic cars in our garage when I was growing up. He spent three years working on a '67 Mustang. He painted it this deep, cherry red. The smell of oil and paint always reminds me of him... but anyway, no, I haven't had any trouble knowing where I am or anything like that. I'm totally oriented.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad or down. I mean, everyone has their moments, but no, not most of the day or anything like that. I actually try to keep a pretty positive outlook. I have this little daily affirmations calendar on my desk at work that really helps set the tone for the morning. It's one of those page-a-day ones, with pictures of national parks. Today's was Yosemite. I went there once, years ago, on a camping trip with some friends from grad school. We pitched our tents right in the valley, and waking up to see El Capitan in the morning light... it was just breathtaking. Though, honestly, I'm not much of a camper anymore. Sleeping on the ground really does a number on your back once you get past forty. My friend Sarah, who went on that trip with us, she actually ended up becoming a park ranger out in Colorado. She sends me a postcard every Christmas with a different mountain peak on it. I have them all pinned up on a corkboard in my kitchen. The corkboard is framed in this really pretty reclaimed barn wood that I found at a flea market upstate... but um, yeah, I'm not feeling sad. My mood has been fine, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost your appetite?\n\nPatient: Appetite? No, not really. I mean, I'm still eating my normal meals. I usually make a turkey sandwich for lunch to take to the clinic. I like to use that thick-cut whole wheat bread, the kind with all the seeds in it? It's so much better than that flimsy white bread that just turns to mush the second you put a slice of tomato on it. I actually grew my own tomatoes last summer in these big terracotta pots on my balcony. They were those little cherry tomatoes, the Sun Gold ones, so sweet you could just eat them like candy. I had this one pot that just went crazy, vines spilling everywhere. I had to tie them up to the railing with some twine. I buy my twine from this little hardware store down the street, it's one of those great old places where the floors creak and they have every size of screw sorted into these tiny little wooden drawers. The guy who owns it, Mr. Henderson, he's been there since the seventies. He always gives my dog a treat when we walk by. He keeps a jar of milk bones right by the register... but yeah, I'm still eating fine. My appetite hasn't changed.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid these symptoms begin after a stressful life event or major adjustment in your life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Stressful event? No, I don't think so. I mean, my life is pretty steady right now. The clinic where I work, it's a routine, you know? I've been a social worker for... gosh, going on twenty years now. I started out working with kids in the foster system, which was incredibly rewarding but definitely burns you out. I had this one supervisor back then, Brenda, she was just a force of nature. She used to wear these bright, chunky necklaces, like turquoise and coral, always clacking when she walked down the hall. She told me she bought them all on her trips to Santa Fe. I've always wanted to go to New Mexico. The light out there is supposed to be incredible for painting. I took a watercolor class at the community center a few months ago, just trying to pick up a new hobby. I tried painting this bowl of lemons I had on my kitchen counter, but getting the yellow right without making it look muddy is so much harder than it looks. The instructor, Dave, said I was using too much water on the brush. He was this very soft-spoken guy, always had paint on his cuffs... but anyway, no, nothing major or stressful has happened recently. Just the usual day-to-day stuff.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... what finally brought me in is that I feel like the part of me that used to reach out for people, or take pleasure in things, has just gone very quiet. I used to love my work, you know? I'm a social worker, and I've always felt this deep connection to helping others navigate their own struggles. It felt vital. And yoga, too. I used to go to this studio downtown, right above a bakery, and the smell of the sourdough would drift up during shavasana. It was wonderful. I even loved going to art galleries and journaling every evening. I have stacks of journals in my apartment, these beautiful leather-bound ones I found at a flea market years ago. The vendor was this lovely older man who told me stories about his travels in Morocco... but anyway, lately, I’m mostly just moving through obligations. Just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Has this loss of interest and pleasure been going on most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: Two years? Oh, no, not that long. It’s really been about eight months. That's when this whole change in how I’m thinking, working, and connecting with people really started. It was right around the time we took my older relative into our apartment. We had to clear out the spare room for her, which used to be my study. I had all my social work textbooks in there, and this beautiful spider plant that I’ve kept alive since grad school. I had to move the plant to the kitchen, and it’s just not getting the same light there, you know? The leaves are turning a bit yellow at the tips, which reminds me I need to buy some of that liquid fertilizer. The hardware store down the street sells it, but the owner is always closing early on Tuesdays for some reason. He says it's for inventory, but I always see him walking his golden retriever in the park on Tuesday afternoons. Beautiful dog, really... but yes, anyway, it hasn't been two years. Just about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Did these mood and interest changes start after you began a new medication, substance, or after a medical illness?\n\nPatient: No, no new medications or anything like that. I mean, I take a daily multivitamin, but I've been taking the same brand for years. I always buy it at the independent pharmacy on the corner, the one with the vintage neon sign in the window? It buzzes a little bit when it's raining outside. The pharmacist there, Brenda, she always wears these incredible hand-knit cardigans. She told me she actually sources the yarn herself from a women's cooperative in Peru, which I just think is so wonderful. Supporting fair trade is so important... in social work, you really see the impact of economic disparities firsthand, so I try to be mindful of where I shop. I’ve always wanted to visit Peru, actually, maybe hike the Inca Trail, but I read that the altitude sickness can be absolutely brutal. \n\nBut, um... no, I haven't been physically ill myself. And I don't use any substances or anything. I mostly just drink chamomile tea in the evenings. I have this beautiful ceramic mug with little blue flowers painted on it that I use every night... but yeah, no new medicines or sickness.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any medical or neurological condition, such as thyroid disease, Parkinson’s disease, stroke, or multiple sclerosis?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I've never had any thyroid issues or anything neurological. I mean, my aunt had some thyroid trouble a few years back, and she had to completely change her diet. She started eating all these strange seaweeds and things, which she bought from this tiny little health food store near her house. The owner of that store actually used to be a jazz musician, if you can believe it. He played the saxophone, and he'd sometimes just play right there in the aisles while you were browsing the vitamins. It was quite an experience, really. I always thought it would be wonderful to learn an instrument, maybe the cello? It just has such a mournful, beautiful sound. I saw a cellist playing in the subway once, right at the 34th Street station, and the acoustics down there just made the notes linger in the air... but no, my own health has been fine. No strokes or Parkinson's or anything like that. Just the mood changes I was telling you about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more, sleeping less, or having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Well, I haven't really noticed any big changes with my sleep. I mean, I go to bed around the same time, usually after I finish my chamomile tea. Sometimes I'll read a little bit before turning off the light. Right now I'm reading this fascinating biography of Eleanor Roosevelt. She was just such an incredible advocate, you know? The way she used her position to champion human rights... it really makes you think about the impact one person can have. It reminds me of why I got into social work in the first place. I actually met a woman at a conference once who had met Eleanor when she was a little girl. She said she had the warmest smile. The conference was in Chicago, right near the lake, and the wind coming off that water in November is just piercing. I had to buy a completely new scarf while I was there, this lovely thick wool one, plum colored. I still wear it when it gets really cold here. But um, yeah, my sleep has been pretty normal, I think. I haven't been sleeping more or less than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had periods where your thinking felt noticeably slower or more foggy than usual?\n\nPatient: Foggy? No, I don't think so. My mind feels pretty clear, honestly. I actually do the crossword puzzle every morning while I have my tea, just to keep everything sharp. I use a fountain pen for it, which my coworker David says is incredibly arrogant of me. He has this very dry sense of humor... he actually used to do stand-up comedy at these little clubs down in the Village back in the nineties. He brought in a VHS tape of one of his old sets once to show us in the breakroom. It's so funny to see those old tapes, you know? It really takes you back. I remember if the VCR ate the ribbon, you had to sit there and carefully wind it all back in with a pencil. I still keep a bunch of pencils on my desk at the clinic in this little ceramic cup shaped like an owl. My niece made it for me when she was in middle school. She's just so talented with her hands, she's actually studying architecture now up in Boston. The winters there are just brutal, though, much worse than here, with all that historic brick just freezing through. But... wait, I'm sorry, what were you asking? Oh, right, my thinking. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Depressed? For two weeks? No, I don't think I've felt like that. I mean, everyone gets a little down sometimes, but not for weeks on end. I usually try to keep my spirits up by listening to music when I'm feeling a bit blah. I have this old record player I found at a flea market a few years ago. It's one of those beautiful wooden ones from the sixties, a Zenith, I think. The speakers are built right into the cabinet. I had to get a new needle for it, which was surprisingly hard to find. I ended up ordering it from this specialty shop in Chicago. The guy I talked to on the phone, Arthur, he knew everything there is to know about vintage audio equipment. He was telling me about how he used to go to all these jazz clubs in the city back in the day. It sounded so glamorous, you know, the smoky rooms and the live bands. I've always loved jazz, especially Ella Fitzgerald. Her voice is just like velvet. I actually tried to sing one of her songs at a karaoke night once, with some friends from the clinic. It was a complete disaster, I totally missed the high notes. My friend Sarah was laughing so hard she spilled her drink on the table. It was this really sticky margarita, and we had to ask the waitress for extra napkins... but no, I haven't been feeling depressed or down like that. Just my usual self.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F34.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... what finally brought me in is that I feel like the part of me that used to reach out for people, or take pleasure in things, has just gone very quiet. I used to love my work, you know? I'm a social worker, and I've always felt this deep connection to helping others navigate their own struggles. It felt vital. And yoga, too. I used to go to this studio downtown, right above a bakery, and the smell of the sourdough would drift up during shavasana. It was wonderful. I even loved going to art galleries and journaling every evening. I have stacks of journals in my apartment, these beautiful leather-bound ones I found at a flea market years ago. The vendor was this lovely older man who told me stories about his travels in Morocco... but anyway, lately, I’m mostly just moving through obligations. Just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Has this loss of interest and pleasure been going on most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: Two years? Oh, no, not that long. It’s really been about eight months. That's when this whole change in how I’m thinking, working, and connecting with people really started. It was right around the time we took my older relative into our apartment. We had to clear out the spare room for her, which used to be my study. I had all my social work textbooks in there, and this beautiful spider plant that I’ve kept alive since grad school. I had to move the plant to the kitchen, and it’s just not getting the same light there, you know? The leaves are turning a bit yellow at the tips, which reminds me I need to buy some of that liquid fertilizer. The hardware store down the street sells it, but the owner is always closing early on Tuesdays for some reason. He says it's for inventory, but I always see him walking his golden retriever in the park on Tuesday afternoons. Beautiful dog, really... but yes, anyway, it hasn't been two years. Just about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Did these mood and interest changes start after you began a new medication, substance, or after a medical illness?\n\nPatient: No, no new medications or anything like that. I mean, I take a daily multivitamin, but I've been taking the same brand for years. I always buy it at the independent pharmacy on the corner, the one with the vintage neon sign in the window? It buzzes a little bit when it's raining outside. The pharmacist there, Brenda, she always wears these incredible hand-knit cardigans. She told me she actually sources the yarn herself from a women's cooperative in Peru, which I just think is so wonderful. Supporting fair trade is so important... in social work, you really see the impact of economic disparities firsthand, so I try to be mindful of where I shop. I’ve always wanted to visit Peru, actually, maybe hike the Inca Trail, but I read that the altitude sickness can be absolutely brutal. \n\nBut, um... no, I haven't been physically ill myself. And I don't use any substances or anything. I mostly just drink chamomile tea in the evenings. I have this beautiful ceramic mug with little blue flowers painted on it that I use every night... but yeah, no new medicines or sickness.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any medical or neurological condition, such as thyroid disease, Parkinson’s disease, stroke, or multiple sclerosis?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I've never had any thyroid issues or anything neurological. I mean, my aunt had some thyroid trouble a few years back, and she had to completely change her diet. She started eating all these strange seaweeds and things, which she bought from this tiny little health food store near her house. The owner of that store actually used to be a jazz musician, if you can believe it. He played the saxophone, and he'd sometimes just play right there in the aisles while you were browsing the vitamins. It was quite an experience, really. I always thought it would be wonderful to learn an instrument, maybe the cello? It just has such a mournful, beautiful sound. I saw a cellist playing in the subway once, right at the 34th Street station, and the acoustics down there just made the notes linger in the air... but no, my own health has been fine. No strokes or Parkinson's or anything like that. Just the mood changes I was telling you about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more, sleeping less, or having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Well, I haven't really noticed any big changes with my sleep. I mean, I go to bed around the same time, usually after I finish my chamomile tea. Sometimes I'll read a little bit before turning off the light. Right now I'm reading this fascinating biography of Eleanor Roosevelt. She was just such an incredible advocate, you know? The way she used her position to champion human rights... it really makes you think about the impact one person can have. It reminds me of why I got into social work in the first place. I actually met a woman at a conference once who had met Eleanor when she was a little girl. She said she had the warmest smile. The conference was in Chicago, right near the lake, and the wind coming off that water in November is just piercing. I had to buy a completely new scarf while I was there, this lovely thick wool one, plum colored. I still wear it when it gets really cold here. But um, yeah, my sleep has been pretty normal, I think. I haven't been sleeping more or less than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had periods where your thinking felt noticeably slower or more foggy than usual?\n\nPatient: Foggy? No, I don't think so. My mind feels pretty clear, honestly. I actually do the crossword puzzle every morning while I have my tea, just to keep everything sharp. I use a fountain pen for it, which my coworker David says is incredibly arrogant of me. He has this very dry sense of humor... he actually used to do stand-up comedy at these little clubs down in the Village back in the nineties. He brought in a VHS tape of one of his old sets once to show us in the breakroom. It's so funny to see those old tapes, you know? It really takes you back. I remember if the VCR ate the ribbon, you had to sit there and carefully wind it all back in with a pencil. I still keep a bunch of pencils on my desk at the clinic in this little ceramic cup shaped like an owl. My niece made it for me when she was in middle school. She's just so talented with her hands, she's actually studying architecture now up in Boston. The winters there are just brutal, though, much worse than here, with all that historic brick just freezing through. But... wait, I'm sorry, what were you asking? Oh, right, my thinking. No, no slowness or fog or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of confusion or trouble knowing where you are?\n\nPatient: Confusion? No, I always know exactly where I am. I've got a pretty good sense of direction, actually. Even when I first moved to the city, I never really got lost. I used to just walk for hours on the weekends, exploring all the different neighborhoods. I found this amazing little bakery down in Little Italy once—just the most incredible cannolis you've ever had. The pastry cream was so light, not heavy and cloying like some places make it. The woman who ran it was originally from Naples, and she told me all about how her grandmother taught her the recipe. She had these beautiful black and white photographs of her family on the wall behind the counter. One of them was of her grandfather standing next to this classic vintage car... I think it was an Alfa Romeo? I've always loved those old cars, the curves on them are just like art. My dad used to restore classic cars in our garage when I was growing up. He spent three years working on a '67 Mustang. He painted it this deep, cherry red. The smell of oil and paint always reminds me of him... but anyway, no, I haven't had any trouble knowing where I am or anything like that. I'm totally oriented.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad or down. I mean, everyone has their moments, but no, not most of the day or anything like that. I actually try to keep a pretty positive outlook. I have this little daily affirmations calendar on my desk at work that really helps set the tone for the morning. It's one of those page-a-day ones, with pictures of national parks. Today's was Yosemite. I went there once, years ago, on a camping trip with some friends from grad school. We pitched our tents right in the valley, and waking up to see El Capitan in the morning light... it was just breathtaking. Though, honestly, I'm not much of a camper anymore. Sleeping on the ground really does a number on your back once you get past forty. My friend Sarah, who went on that trip with us, she actually ended up becoming a park ranger out in Colorado. She sends me a postcard every Christmas with a different mountain peak on it. I have them all pinned up on a corkboard in my kitchen. The corkboard is framed in this really pretty reclaimed barn wood that I found at a flea market upstate... but um, yeah, I'm not feeling sad. My mood has been fine, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost your appetite?\n\nPatient: Appetite? No, not really. I mean, I'm still eating my normal meals. I usually make a turkey sandwich for lunch to take to the clinic. I like to use that thick-cut whole wheat bread, the kind with all the seeds in it? It's so much better than that flimsy white bread that just turns to mush the second you put a slice of tomato on it. I actually grew my own tomatoes last summer in these big terracotta pots on my balcony. They were those little cherry tomatoes, the Sun Gold ones, so sweet you could just eat them like candy. I had this one pot that just went crazy, vines spilling everywhere. I had to tie them up to the railing with some twine. I buy my twine from this little hardware store down the street, it's one of those great old places where the floors creak and they have every size of screw sorted into these tiny little wooden drawers. The guy who owns it, Mr. Henderson, he's been there since the seventies. He always gives my dog a treat when we walk by. He keeps a jar of milk bones right by the register... but yeah, I'm still eating fine. My appetite hasn't changed.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms begin after a stressful life event or major adjustment in your life?\n\nPatient: Stressful event? No, I don't think so. I mean, my life is pretty steady right now. The clinic where I work, it's a routine, you know? I've been a social worker for... gosh, going on twenty years now. I started out working with kids in the foster system, which was incredibly rewarding but definitely burns you out. I had this one supervisor back then, Brenda, she was just a force of nature. She used to wear these bright, chunky necklaces, like turquoise and coral, always clacking when she walked down the hall. She told me she bought them all on her trips to Santa Fe. I've always wanted to go to New Mexico. The light out there is supposed to be incredible for painting. I took a watercolor class at the community center a few months ago, just trying to pick up a new hobby. I tried painting this bowl of lemons I had on my kitchen counter, but getting the yellow right without making it look muddy is so much harder than it looks. The instructor, Dave, said I was using too much water on the brush. He was this very soft-spoken guy, always had paint on his cuffs... but anyway, no, nothing major or stressful has happened recently. Just the usual day-to-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energetic or 'up' and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Unusually energetic? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I usually need my solid eight hours of sleep, otherwise I'm just dragging the next day. I've always been a pretty good sleeper, actually. I bought this new mattress last year, one of those memory foam ones that comes rolled up in a box? It was so heavy getting it up the stairs to my bedroom. I had to ask my neighbor, Paul, to help me. He's such a nice guy, always tinkering with his vintage motorcycle in the driveway. It's a Triumph, I think. A really beautiful deep green color. He actually repainted it himself in his garage. He showed me the whole setup he has, with the air compressor and the spray guns. It made me think about how much patience you need for something like that, you know, getting the coats perfectly even without any drips. I don't think I'd have the steady hand for it. I tried to frost a cake for my niece's birthday last month—she turned seven, wanted a mermaid theme—and the icing was just a lumpy disaster. I tried to smooth it out with a warm spatula, which is a trick I saw on a baking show, but it just made the colors bleed together into this weird grayish-blue... but um, no, I haven't had any periods where I felt super energetic or didn't need sleep. Just my normal routine.\n\nDoctor: During the past eight months, have there been at least two weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? For two weeks? No, I don't think I've felt like that. I mean, everyone gets a little down sometimes, but not for weeks on end. I usually try to keep my spirits up by listening to music when I'm feeling a bit blah. I have this old record player I found at a flea market a few years ago. It's one of those beautiful wooden ones from the sixties, a Zenith, I think. The speakers are built right into the cabinet. I had to get a new needle for it, which was surprisingly hard to find. I ended up ordering it from this specialty shop in Chicago. The guy I talked to on the phone, Arthur, he knew everything there is to know about vintage audio equipment. He was telling me about how he used to go to all these jazz clubs in the city back in the day. It sounded so glamorous, you know, the smoky rooms and the live bands. I've always loved jazz, especially Ella Fitzgerald. Her voice is just like velvet. I actually tried to sing one of her songs at a karaoke night once, with some friends from the clinic. It was a complete disaster, I totally missed the high notes. My friend Sarah was laughing so hard she spilled her drink on the table. It was this really sticky margarita, and we had to ask the waitress for extra napkins... but no, I haven't been feeling depressed or down like that. Just my usual self.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It made me think about how much patience you need for something like that, you know, getting the coats perfectly even without any drips. I don't think I'd have the steady hand for it. I tried to frost a cake for my niece's birthday last month—she turned seven, wanted a mermaid theme—and the icing was just a lumpy disaster. I tried to smooth it out with a warm spatula, which is a trick I saw on a baking show, but it just made the colors bleed together into this weird grayish-blue... but um, no, I haven't had any periods where I felt super energetic or didn't need sleep. Just my normal routine.\n\nDoctor: During the past eight months, have there been at least two weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? For two weeks? No, I don't think I've felt like that. I mean, everyone gets a little down sometimes, but not for weeks on end. I usually try to keep my spirits up by listening to music when I'm feeling a bit blah. I have this old record player I found at a flea market a few years ago. It's one of those beautiful wooden ones from the sixties, a Zenith, I think. The speakers are built right into the cabinet. I had to get a new needle for it, which was surprisingly hard to find. I ended up ordering it from this specialty shop in Chicago. The guy I talked to on the phone, Arthur, he knew everything there is to know about vintage audio equipment. He was telling me about how he used to go to all these jazz clubs in the city back in the day. It sounded so glamorous, you know, the smoky rooms and the live bands. I've always loved jazz, especially Ella Fitzgerald. Her voice is just like velvet. I actually tried to sing one of her songs at a karaoke night once, with some friends from the clinic. It was a complete disaster, I totally missed the high notes. My friend Sarah was laughing so hard she spilled her drink on the table. It was this really sticky margarita, and we had to ask the waitress for extra napkins... but no, I haven't been feeling depressed or down like that. Just my usual self.\n\nDoctor: Over the past eight months, have you had days when you felt sad or down most of the day?\n\nPatient: Sad most of the day? No, I don't think so. I mean, I have days where I'm just tired, but not really sad. When I get home from work, I usually just want to put my feet up and watch something on TV. I've been watching this documentary series about the history of the postal service, which sounds boring, I know, but it's actually fascinating. Did you know they used to have these pneumatic tubes under the streets in New York City to send mail? Just these little canisters shooting around underground. It makes you think about how much infrastructure is hidden right beneath our feet, you know? Like the subway systems. I took the subway when I visited my sister in Boston last fall. She lives in Somerville, right near this amazing bakery that makes the best almond croissants. We went there every single morning. The baker, this older woman named Maria, she remembered our order by the third day. She was telling us how her family brought the recipe over from Portugal. I've always wanted to visit Europe, maybe take a train through the countryside... but um, what were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling sad. No, I haven't had days like that. Just regular days.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the past eight months, have you felt depressed or down more days than not?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / mental_health_or_substance_abuse_social_worker / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
More days than not? Um, no. I don't think so. I mean, I have my moments, but definitely not more days than not. I try to stay busy, you know? Keeping busy helps. I actually started organizing my bookshelf by color last weekend. It's this whole trend I saw online, the rainbow bookshelf? It looks really striking once you get it done. But it's surprisingly hard because a lot of book spines aren't just one solid color. Like, I have this old copy of Jane Eyre, and the spine is mostly blue but with these gold letters and a little picture of a house that's sort of brownish-red. So I didn't know whether to put it with the blues or the browns. I ended up putting it with the blues next to my encyclopedias. I bought this set of encyclopedias at a garage sale years ago, before the internet was really a thing. They're bound in this beautiful navy blue leather. The woman selling them said they belonged to her grandfather who was a professor. It made me wonder what he taught. Maybe history? I love history. I was just reading about the history of the zipper the other day... but anyway, no, I haven't felt depressed more days than not.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F34.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... what finally brought me in is that I feel like the part of me that used to reach out for people, or take pleasure in things, has just gone very quiet. I used to love my work, you know? I'm a social worker, and I've always felt this deep connection to helping others navigate their own struggles. It felt vital. And yoga, too. I used to go to this studio downtown, right above a bakery, and the smell of the sourdough would drift up during shavasana. It was wonderful. I even loved going to art galleries and journaling every evening. I have stacks of journals in my apartment, these beautiful leather-bound ones I found at a flea market years ago. The vendor was this lovely older man who told me stories about his travels in Morocco... but anyway, lately, I’m mostly just moving through obligations. Just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Has this loss of interest and pleasure been going on most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: Two years? Oh, no, not that long. It’s really been about eight months. That's when this whole change in how I’m thinking, working, and connecting with people really started. It was right around the time we took my older relative into our apartment. We had to clear out the spare room for her, which used to be my study. I had all my social work textbooks in there, and this beautiful spider plant that I’ve kept alive since grad school. I had to move the plant to the kitchen, and it’s just not getting the same light there, you know? The leaves are turning a bit yellow at the tips, which reminds me I need to buy some of that liquid fertilizer. The hardware store down the street sells it, but the owner is always closing early on Tuesdays for some reason. I mean, I take a daily multivitamin, but I've been taking the same brand for years. I always buy it at the independent pharmacy on the corner, the one with the vintage neon sign in the window? It buzzes a little bit when it's raining outside. The pharmacist there, Brenda, she always wears these incredible hand-knit cardigans. She told me she actually sources the yarn herself from a women's cooperative in Peru, which I just think is so wonderful. Supporting fair trade is so important... in social work, you really see the impact of economic disparities firsthand, so I try to be mindful of where I shop.
