I have this beautiful ceramic mug with little blue flowers painted on it that I use every night... but yeah, no new medicines or sickness.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any medical or neurological condition, such as thyroid disease, Parkinson’s disease, stroke, or multiple sclerosis?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I've never had any thyroid issues or anything neurological. I mean, my aunt had some thyroid trouble a few years back, and she had to completely change her diet. She started eating all these strange seaweeds and things, which she bought from this tiny little health food store near her house. The owner of that store actually used to be a jazz musician, if you can believe it. He played the saxophone, and he'd sometimes just play right there in the aisles while you were browsing the vitamins. It was quite an experience, really. I always thought it would be wonderful to learn an instrument, maybe the cello? It just has such a mournful, beautiful sound. I saw a cellist playing in the subway once, right at the 34th Street station, and the acoustics down there just made the notes linger in the air... but no, my own health has been fine. No strokes or Parkinson's or anything like that. Just the mood changes I was telling you about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more, sleeping less, or having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Well, I haven't really noticed any big changes with my sleep. I mean, I go to bed around the same time, usually after I finish my chamomile tea. Sometimes I'll read a little bit before turning off the light. Right now I'm reading this fascinating biography of Eleanor Roosevelt. She was just such an incredible advocate, you know? The way she used her position to champion human rights... it really makes you think about the impact one person can have. It reminds me of why I got into social work in the first place. I actually met a woman at a conference once who had met Eleanor when she was a little girl. She said she had the warmest smile. The conference was in Chicago, right near the lake, and the wind coming off that water in November is just piercing. I had to buy a completely new scarf while I was there, this lovely thick wool one, plum colored. I still wear it when it gets really cold here. But um, yeah, my sleep has been pretty normal, I think. I haven't been sleeping more or less than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had periods where your thinking felt noticeably slower or more foggy than usual?\n\nPatient: Foggy? No, I don't think so. My mind feels pretty clear, honestly. I actually do the crossword puzzle every morning while I have my tea, just to keep everything sharp. I use a fountain pen for it, which my coworker David says is incredibly arrogant of me. He has this very dry sense of humor... he actually used to do stand-up comedy at these little clubs down in the Village back in the nineties. He brought in a VHS tape of one of his old sets once to show us in the breakroom. It's so funny to see those old tapes, you know? It really takes you back. I remember if the VCR ate the ribbon, you had to sit there and carefully wind it all back in with a pencil. I still keep a bunch of pencils on my desk at the clinic in this little ceramic cup shaped like an owl. My niece made it for me when she was in middle school. She's just so talented with her hands, she's actually studying architecture now up in Boston. The winters there are just brutal, though, much worse than here, with all that historic brick just freezing through. But... wait, I'm sorry, what were you asking? Oh, right, my thinking. No, no slowness or fog or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of confusion or trouble knowing where you are?\n\nPatient: Confusion? No, I always know exactly where I am. I've got a pretty good sense of direction, actually. Even when I first moved to the city, I never really got lost. I used to just walk for hours on the weekends, exploring all the different neighborhoods. I found this amazing little bakery down in Little Italy once—just the most incredible cannolis you've ever had. The pastry cream was so light, not heavy and cloying like some places make it. The woman who ran it was originally from Naples, and she told me all about how her grandmother taught her the recipe. She had these beautiful black and white photographs of her family on the wall behind the counter. One of them was of her grandfather standing next to this classic vintage car... I think it was an Alfa Romeo? I've always loved those old cars, the curves on them are just like art. My dad used to restore classic cars in our garage when I was growing up. He spent three years working on a '67 Mustang. He painted it this deep, cherry red. The smell of oil and paint always reminds me of him... but anyway, no, I haven't had any trouble knowing where I am or anything like that. I'm totally oriented.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad or down. I mean, everyone has their moments, but no, not most of the day or anything like that. I actually try to keep a pretty positive outlook. I have this little daily affirmations calendar on my desk at work that really helps set the tone for the morning. It's one of those page-a-day ones, with pictures of national parks. Today's was Yosemite. I went there once, years ago, on a camping trip with some friends from grad school. We pitched our tents right in the valley, and waking up to see El Capitan in the morning light... it was just breathtaking. Though, honestly, I'm not much of a camper anymore. Sleeping on the ground really does a number on your back once you get past forty. My friend Sarah, who went on that trip with us, she actually ended up becoming a park ranger out in Colorado. She sends me a postcard every Christmas with a different mountain peak on it. I have them all pinned up on a corkboard in my kitchen. The corkboard is framed in this really pretty reclaimed barn wood that I found at a flea market upstate... but um, yeah, I'm not feeling sad. My mood has been fine, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost your appetite?\n\nPatient: Appetite? No, not really. I mean, I'm still eating my normal meals. I usually make a turkey sandwich for lunch to take to the clinic. I like to use that thick-cut whole wheat bread, the kind with all the seeds in it? It's so much better than that flimsy white bread that just turns to mush the second you put a slice of tomato on it. I actually grew my own tomatoes last summer in these big terracotta pots on my balcony. They were those little cherry tomatoes, the Sun Gold ones, so sweet you could just eat them like candy. I had this one pot that just went crazy, vines spilling everywhere. I had to tie them up to the railing with some twine. I buy my twine from this little hardware store down the street, it's one of those great old places where the floors creak and they have every size of screw sorted into these tiny little wooden drawers. The guy who owns it, Mr. Henderson, he's been there since the seventies. He always gives my dog a treat when we walk by. He keeps a jar of milk bones right by the register... but yeah, I'm still eating fine. My appetite hasn't changed.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms begin after a stressful life event or major adjustment in your life?\n\nPatient: Stressful event? No, I don't think so. I mean, my life is pretty steady right now. The clinic where I work, it's a routine, you know? I've been a social worker for... gosh, going on twenty years now. I started out working with kids in the foster system, which was incredibly rewarding but definitely burns you out. I had this one supervisor back then, Brenda, she was just a force of nature. She used to wear these bright, chunky necklaces, like turquoise and coral, always clacking when she walked down the hall. She told me she bought them all on her trips to Santa Fe. I've always wanted to go to New Mexico. The light out there is supposed to be incredible for painting. I took a watercolor class at the community center a few months ago, just trying to pick up a new hobby. I tried painting this bowl of lemons I had on my kitchen counter, but getting the yellow right without making it look muddy is so much harder than it looks. The instructor, Dave, said I was using too much water on the brush. He was this very soft-spoken guy, always had paint on his cuffs... but anyway, no, nothing major or stressful has happened recently. Just the usual day-to-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energetic or 'up' and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Unusually energetic? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I usually need my solid eight hours of sleep, otherwise I'm just dragging the next day. I've always been a pretty good sleeper, actually. I bought this new mattress last year, one of those memory foam ones that comes rolled up in a box? It was so heavy getting it up the stairs to my bedroom. I had to ask my neighbor, Paul, to help me. He's such a nice guy, always tinkering with his vintage motorcycle in the driveway. It's a Triumph, I think. A really beautiful deep green color. He actually repainted it himself in his garage. He showed me the whole setup he has, with the air compressor and the spray guns. It made me think about how much patience you need for something like that, you know, getting the coats perfectly even without any drips. I don't think I'd have the steady hand for it. I tried to frost a cake for my niece's birthday last month—she turned seven, wanted a mermaid theme—and the icing was just a lumpy disaster. I tried to smooth it out with a warm spatula, which is a trick I saw on a baking show, but it just made the colors bleed together into this weird grayish-blue... but um, no, I haven't had any periods where I felt super energetic or didn't need sleep. Just my normal routine.\n\nDoctor: During the past eight months, have there been at least two weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? For two weeks? No, I don't think I've felt like that. I mean, everyone gets a little down sometimes, but not for weeks on end. I usually try to keep my spirits up by listening to music when I'm feeling a bit blah. I have this old record player I found at a flea market a few years ago. It's one of those beautiful wooden ones from the sixties, a Zenith, I think. The speakers are built right into the cabinet. I had to get a new needle for it, which was surprisingly hard to find. I ended up ordering it from this specialty shop in Chicago. The guy I talked to on the phone, Arthur, he knew everything there is to know about vintage audio equipment. He was telling me about how he used to go to all these jazz clubs in the city back in the day. It sounded so glamorous, you know, the smoky rooms and the live bands. I've always loved jazz, especially Ella Fitzgerald. Her voice is just like velvet. I actually tried to sing one of her songs at a karaoke night once, with some friends from the clinic. It was a complete disaster, I totally missed the high notes. My friend Sarah was laughing so hard she spilled her drink on the table. It was this really sticky margarita, and we had to ask the waitress for extra napkins... but no, I haven't been feeling depressed or down like that. Just my usual self.\n\nDoctor: Over the past eight months, have you had days when you felt sad or down most of the day?\n\nPatient: Sad most of the day? No, I don't think so. I mean, I have days where I'm just tired, but not really sad. When I get home from work, I usually just want to put my feet up and watch something on TV. I've been watching this documentary series about the history of the postal service, which sounds boring, I know, but it's actually fascinating. The winters there are just brutal, though, much worse than here, with all that historic brick just freezing through. But... wait, I'm sorry, what were you asking? Oh, right, my thinking. No, no slowness or fog or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of confusion or trouble knowing where you are?\n\nPatient: Confusion? No, I always know exactly where I am. I've got a pretty good sense of direction, actually. Even when I first moved to the city, I never really got lost. I used to just walk for hours on the weekends, exploring all the different neighborhoods. I found this amazing little bakery down in Little Italy once—just the most incredible cannolis you've ever had. The pastry cream was so light, not heavy and cloying like some places make it. The woman who ran it was originally from Naples, and she told me all about how her grandmother taught her the recipe. She had these beautiful black and white photographs of her family on the wall behind the counter. One of them was of her grandfather standing next to this classic vintage car... I think it was an Alfa Romeo? I've always loved those old cars, the curves on them are just like art. My dad used to restore classic cars in our garage when I was growing up. He spent three years working on a '67 Mustang. He painted it this deep, cherry red. The smell of oil and paint always reminds me of him... but anyway, no, I haven't had any trouble knowing where I am or anything like that. I'm totally oriented.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad or down. I mean, everyone has their moments, but no, not most of the day or anything like that. I actually try to keep a pretty positive outlook. I have this little daily affirmations calendar on my desk at work that really helps set the tone for the morning. It's one of those page-a-day ones, with pictures of national parks. Today's was Yosemite. I went there once, years ago, on a camping trip with some friends from grad school. We pitched our tents right in the valley, and waking up to see El Capitan in the morning light... it was just breathtaking. Though, honestly, I'm not much of a camper anymore. Sleeping on the ground really does a number on your back once you get past forty. My friend Sarah, who went on that trip with us, she actually ended up becoming a park ranger out in Colorado. She sends me a postcard every Christmas with a different mountain peak on it. I have them all pinned up on a corkboard in my kitchen. The corkboard is framed in this really pretty reclaimed barn wood that I found at a flea market upstate... but um, yeah, I'm not feeling sad. My mood has been fine, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost your appetite?\n\nPatient: Appetite? No, not really. I mean, I'm still eating my normal meals. I usually make a turkey sandwich for lunch to take to the clinic. I like to use that thick-cut whole wheat bread, the kind with all the seeds in it? It's so much better than that flimsy white bread that just turns to mush the second you put a slice of tomato on it. I actually grew my own tomatoes last summer in these big terracotta pots on my balcony. They were those little cherry tomatoes, the Sun Gold ones, so sweet you could just eat them like candy. I had this one pot that just went crazy, vines spilling everywhere. I had to tie them up to the railing with some twine. I buy my twine from this little hardware store down the street, it's one of those great old places where the floors creak and they have every size of screw sorted into these tiny little wooden drawers. The guy who owns it, Mr. Henderson, he's been there since the seventies. He always gives my dog a treat when we walk by. He keeps a jar of milk bones right by the register... but yeah, I'm still eating fine. My appetite hasn't changed.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms begin after a stressful life event or major adjustment in your life?\n\nPatient: Stressful event? No, I don't think so. I mean, my life is pretty steady right now. The clinic where I work, it's a routine, you know? I've been a social worker for... gosh, going on twenty years now. I started out working with kids in the foster system, which was incredibly rewarding but definitely burns you out. I had this one supervisor back then, Brenda, she was just a force of nature. She used to wear these bright, chunky necklaces, like turquoise and coral, always clacking when she walked down the hall. She told me she bought them all on her trips to Santa Fe. I've always wanted to go to New Mexico. The light out there is supposed to be incredible for painting. I took a watercolor class at the community center a few months ago, just trying to pick up a new hobby. I tried painting this bowl of lemons I had on my kitchen counter, but getting the yellow right without making it look muddy is so much harder than it looks. The instructor, Dave, said I was using too much water on the brush. He was this very soft-spoken guy, always had paint on his cuffs... but anyway, no, nothing major or stressful has happened recently. Just the usual day-to-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energetic or 'up' and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Unusually energetic? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I usually need my solid eight hours of sleep, otherwise I'm just dragging the next day. I've always been a pretty good sleeper, actually. I bought this new mattress last year, one of those memory foam ones that comes rolled up in a box? It was so heavy getting it up the stairs to my bedroom. I had to ask my neighbor, Paul, to help me. He's such a nice guy, always tinkering with his vintage motorcycle in the driveway. It's a Triumph, I think. A really beautiful deep green color. He actually repainted it himself in his garage. He showed me the whole setup he has, with the air compressor and the spray guns. It made me think about how much patience you need for something like that, you know, getting the coats perfectly even without any drips. I don't think I'd have the steady hand for it. I tried to frost a cake for my niece's birthday last month—she turned seven, wanted a mermaid theme—and the icing was just a lumpy disaster. I tried to smooth it out with a warm spatula, which is a trick I saw on a baking show, but it just made the colors bleed together into this weird grayish-blue... but um, no, I haven't had any periods where I felt super energetic or didn't need sleep. Just my normal routine.\n\nDoctor: During the past eight months, have there been at least two weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? For two weeks? No, I don't think I've felt like that. I mean, everyone gets a little down sometimes, but not for weeks on end. I usually try to keep my spirits up by listening to music when I'm feeling a bit blah. I have this old record player I found at a flea market a few years ago. It's one of those beautiful wooden ones from the sixties, a Zenith, I think. The speakers are built right into the cabinet. I had to get a new needle for it, which was surprisingly hard to find. I ended up ordering it from this specialty shop in Chicago. The guy I talked to on the phone, Arthur, he knew everything there is to know about vintage audio equipment. He was telling me about how he used to go to all these jazz clubs in the city back in the day. It sounded so glamorous, you know, the smoky rooms and the live bands. I've always loved jazz, especially Ella Fitzgerald. Her voice is just like velvet. I actually tried to sing one of her songs at a karaoke night once, with some friends from the clinic. It was a complete disaster, I totally missed the high notes. My friend Sarah was laughing so hard she spilled her drink on the table. It was this really sticky margarita, and we had to ask the waitress for extra napkins... but no, I haven't been feeling depressed or down like that. Just my usual self.\n\nDoctor: Over the past eight months, have you had days when you felt sad or down most of the day?\n\nPatient: Sad most of the day? No, I don't think so. I mean, I have days where I'm just tired, but not really sad. When I get home from work, I usually just want to put my feet up and watch something on TV. I've been watching this documentary series about the history of the postal service, which sounds boring, I know, but it's actually fascinating. Did you know they used to have these pneumatic tubes under the streets in New York City to send mail? Just these little canisters shooting around underground. It makes you think about how much infrastructure is hidden right beneath our feet, you know? Like the subway systems. I took the subway when I visited my sister in Boston last fall. She lives in Somerville, right near this amazing bakery that makes the best almond croissants. We went there every single morning. The baker, this older woman named Maria, she remembered our order by the third day. She was telling us how her family brought the recipe over from Portugal. I've always wanted to visit Europe, maybe take a train through the countryside... but um, what were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling sad. No, I haven't had days like that. Just regular days.\n\nDoctor: Over the past eight months, have you felt depressed or down more days than not?\n\nPatient: More days than not? Um, no. I don't think so. I mean, I have my moments, but definitely not more days than not. I try to stay busy, you know? Keeping busy helps. I actually started organizing my bookshelf by color last weekend. It's this whole trend I saw online, the rainbow bookshelf? It looks really striking once you get it done. But it's surprisingly hard because a lot of book spines aren't just one solid color. Like, I have this old copy of Jane Eyre, and the spine is mostly blue but with these gold letters and a little picture of a house that's sort of brownish-red. So I didn't know whether to put it with the blues or the browns. I ended up putting it with the blues next to my encyclopedias. I bought this set of encyclopedias at a garage sale years ago, before the internet was really a thing. They're bound in this beautiful navy blue leather. The woman selling them said they belonged to her grandfather who was a professor. It made me wonder what he taught. Maybe history? I love history. I was just reading about the history of the zipper the other day... but anyway, no, I haven't felt depressed more days than not.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Depressed at all? Um... no, not really. I mean, I guess I have moments where I feel a bit flat, but not depressed. When I feel flat, I usually try to get outside. Fresh air is so important. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... what finally brought me in is that I feel like the part of me that used to reach out for people, or take pleasure in things, has just gone very quiet. I used to love my work, you know? I'm a social worker, and I've always felt this deep connection to helping others navigate their own struggles. It felt vital. And yoga, too. I used to go to this studio downtown, right above a bakery, and the smell of the sourdough would drift up during shavasana. It was wonderful. I even loved going to art galleries and journaling every evening. I have stacks of journals in my apartment, these beautiful leather-bound ones I found at a flea market years ago. The vendor was this lovely older man who told me stories about his travels in Morocco... but anyway, lately, I’m mostly just moving through obligations. Just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Has this loss of interest and pleasure been going on most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: Two years? Oh, no, not that long. It’s really been about eight months. That's when this whole change in how I’m thinking, working, and connecting with people really started. It was right around the time we took my older relative into our apartment. We had to clear out the spare room for her, which used to be my study. I had all my social work textbooks in there, and this beautiful spider plant that I’ve kept alive since grad school. I had to move the plant to the kitchen, and it’s just not getting the same light there, you know? The leaves are turning a bit yellow at the tips, which reminds me I need to buy some of that liquid fertilizer. The hardware store down the street sells it, but the owner is always closing early on Tuesdays for some reason. He says it's for inventory, but I always see him walking his golden retriever in the park on Tuesday afternoons. Beautiful dog, really... but yes, anyway, it hasn't been two years. Just about eight months.\n\nDoctor: Did these mood and interest changes start after you began a new medication, substance, or after a medical illness?\n\nPatient: No, no new medications or anything like that. I mean, I take a daily multivitamin, but I've been taking the same brand for years. I always buy it at the independent pharmacy on the corner, the one with the vintage neon sign in the window? It buzzes a little bit when it's raining outside. The pharmacist there, Brenda, she always wears these incredible hand-knit cardigans. She told me she actually sources the yarn herself from a women's cooperative in Peru, which I just think is so wonderful. Supporting fair trade is so important... in social work, you really see the impact of economic disparities firsthand, so I try to be mindful of where I shop. I’ve always wanted to visit Peru, actually, maybe hike the Inca Trail, but I read that the altitude sickness can be absolutely brutal. \n\nBut, um... no, I haven't been physically ill myself. And I don't use any substances or anything. I mostly just drink chamomile tea in the evenings. I have this beautiful ceramic mug with little blue flowers painted on it that I use every night... but yeah, no new medicines or sickness.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any medical or neurological condition, such as thyroid disease, Parkinson’s disease, stroke, or multiple sclerosis?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I've never had any thyroid issues or anything neurological. I mean, my aunt had some thyroid trouble a few years back, and she had to completely change her diet. She started eating all these strange seaweeds and things, which she bought from this tiny little health food store near her house. The owner of that store actually used to be a jazz musician, if you can believe it. He played the saxophone, and he'd sometimes just play right there in the aisles while you were browsing the vitamins. It was quite an experience, really. I always thought it would be wonderful to learn an instrument, maybe the cello? It just has such a mournful, beautiful sound. I saw a cellist playing in the subway once, right at the 34th Street station, and the acoustics down there just made the notes linger in the air... but no, my own health has been fine. No strokes or Parkinson's or anything like that. Just the mood changes I was telling you about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more, sleeping less, or having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Well, I haven't really noticed any big changes with my sleep. I mean, I go to bed around the same time, usually after I finish my chamomile tea. Sometimes I'll read a little bit before turning off the light. Right now I'm reading this fascinating biography of Eleanor Roosevelt. She was just such an incredible advocate, you know? The way she used her position to champion human rights... it really makes you think about the impact one person can have. It reminds me of why I got into social work in the first place. I actually met a woman at a conference once who had met Eleanor when she was a little girl. She said she had the warmest smile. The conference was in Chicago, right near the lake, and the wind coming off that water in November is just piercing. I had to buy a completely new scarf while I was there, this lovely thick wool one, plum colored. I still wear it when it gets really cold here. But um, yeah, my sleep has been pretty normal, I think. I haven't been sleeping more or less than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had periods where your thinking felt noticeably slower or more foggy than usual?\n\nPatient: Foggy? No, I don't think so. My mind feels pretty clear, honestly. I actually do the crossword puzzle every morning while I have my tea, just to keep everything sharp. I use a fountain pen for it, which my coworker David says is incredibly arrogant of me. He has this very dry sense of humor... he actually used to do stand-up comedy at these little clubs down in the Village back in the nineties. He brought in a VHS tape of one of his old sets once to show us in the breakroom. It's so funny to see those old tapes, you know? It really takes you back. I remember if the VCR ate the ribbon, you had to sit there and carefully wind it all back in with a pencil. I still keep a bunch of pencils on my desk at the clinic in this little ceramic cup shaped like an owl. My niece made it for me when she was in middle school. She's just so talented with her hands, she's actually studying architecture now up in Boston. The winters there are just brutal, though, much worse than here, with all that historic brick just freezing through. But... wait, I'm sorry, what were you asking? Oh, right, my thinking. No, no slowness or fog or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of confusion or trouble knowing where you are?\n\nPatient: Confusion? No, I always know exactly where I am. I've got a pretty good sense of direction, actually. Even when I first moved to the city, I never really got lost. I used to just walk for hours on the weekends, exploring all the different neighborhoods. I found this amazing little bakery down in Little Italy once—just the most incredible cannolis you've ever had. The pastry cream was so light, not heavy and cloying like some places make it. The woman who ran it was originally from Naples, and she told me all about how her grandmother taught her the recipe. She had these beautiful black and white photographs of her family on the wall behind the counter. One of them was of her grandfather standing next to this classic vintage car... I think it was an Alfa Romeo? I've always loved those old cars, the curves on them are just like art. My dad used to restore classic cars in our garage when I was growing up. He spent three years working on a '67 Mustang. He painted it this deep, cherry red. The smell of oil and paint always reminds me of him... but anyway, no, I haven't had any trouble knowing where I am or anything like that. I'm totally oriented.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad or down. I mean, everyone has their moments, but no, not most of the day or anything like that. I actually try to keep a pretty positive outlook. I have this little daily affirmations calendar on my desk at work that really helps set the tone for the morning. It's one of those page-a-day ones, with pictures of national parks. Today's was Yosemite. I went there once, years ago, on a camping trip with some friends from grad school. We pitched our tents right in the valley, and waking up to see El Capitan in the morning light... it was just breathtaking. Though, honestly, I'm not much of a camper anymore. Sleeping on the ground really does a number on your back once you get past forty. My friend Sarah, who went on that trip with us, she actually ended up becoming a park ranger out in Colorado. She sends me a postcard every Christmas with a different mountain peak on it. I have them all pinned up on a corkboard in my kitchen. The corkboard is framed in this really pretty reclaimed barn wood that I found at a flea market upstate... but um, yeah, I'm not feeling sad. My mood has been fine, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost your appetite?\n\nPatient: Appetite? No, not really. I mean, I'm still eating my normal meals. I usually make a turkey sandwich for lunch to take to the clinic. I like to use that thick-cut whole wheat bread, the kind with all the seeds in it? It's so much better than that flimsy white bread that just turns to mush the second you put a slice of tomato on it. I actually grew my own tomatoes last summer in these big terracotta pots on my balcony. They were those little cherry tomatoes, the Sun Gold ones, so sweet you could just eat them like candy. I had this one pot that just went crazy, vines spilling everywhere. I had to tie them up to the railing with some twine. I buy my twine from this little hardware store down the street, it's one of those great old places where the floors creak and they have every size of screw sorted into these tiny little wooden drawers. The guy who owns it, Mr. Henderson, he's been there since the seventies. He always gives my dog a treat when we walk by. He keeps a jar of milk bones right by the register... but yeah, I'm still eating fine. My appetite hasn't changed.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms begin after a stressful life event or major adjustment in your life?\n\nPatient: Stressful event? No, I don't think so. I mean, my life is pretty steady right now. The clinic where I work, it's a routine, you know? I've been a social worker for... gosh, going on twenty years now. I started out working with kids in the foster system, which was incredibly rewarding but definitely burns you out. I had this one supervisor back then, Brenda, she was just a force of nature. She used to wear these bright, chunky necklaces, like turquoise and coral, always clacking when she walked down the hall. She told me she bought them all on her trips to Santa Fe. I've always wanted to go to New Mexico. The light out there is supposed to be incredible for painting. I took a watercolor class at the community center a few months ago, just trying to pick up a new hobby. I tried painting this bowl of lemons I had on my kitchen counter, but getting the yellow right without making it look muddy is so much harder than it looks. The instructor, Dave, said I was using too much water on the brush. He was this very soft-spoken guy, always had paint on his cuffs... but anyway, no, nothing major or stressful has happened recently. Just the usual day-to-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energetic or 'up' and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Unusually energetic? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I usually need my solid eight hours of sleep, otherwise I'm just dragging the next day. I've always been a pretty good sleeper, actually. I bought this new mattress last year, one of those memory foam ones that comes rolled up in a box? It was so heavy getting it up the stairs to my bedroom. I had to ask my neighbor, Paul, to help me. He's such a nice guy, always tinkering with his vintage motorcycle in the driveway. It's a Triumph, I think. A really beautiful deep green color. He actually repainted it himself in his garage. He showed me the whole setup he has, with the air compressor and the spray guns. It made me think about how much patience you need for something like that, you know, getting the coats perfectly even without any drips. I don't think I'd have the steady hand for it. I tried to frost a cake for my niece's birthday last month—she turned seven, wanted a mermaid theme—and the icing was just a lumpy disaster. I tried to smooth it out with a warm spatula, which is a trick I saw on a baking show, but it just made the colors bleed together into this weird grayish-blue... but um, no, I haven't had any periods where I felt super energetic or didn't need sleep. Just my normal routine.\n\nDoctor: During the past eight months, have there been at least two weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? For two weeks? No, I don't think I've felt like that. I mean, everyone gets a little down sometimes, but not for weeks on end. I usually try to keep my spirits up by listening to music when I'm feeling a bit blah. I have this old record player I found at a flea market a few years ago. It's one of those beautiful wooden ones from the sixties, a Zenith, I think. The speakers are built right into the cabinet. I had to get a new needle for it, which was surprisingly hard to find. I ended up ordering it from this specialty shop in Chicago. The guy I talked to on the phone, Arthur, he knew everything there is to know about vintage audio equipment. He was telling me about how he used to go to all these jazz clubs in the city back in the day. It sounded so glamorous, you know, the smoky rooms and the live bands. I've always loved jazz, especially Ella Fitzgerald. Her voice is just like velvet. I actually tried to sing one of her songs at a karaoke night once, with some friends from the clinic. It was a complete disaster, I totally missed the high notes. My friend Sarah was laughing so hard she spilled her drink on the table. It was this really sticky margarita, and we had to ask the waitress for extra napkins... but no, I haven't been feeling depressed or down like that. Just my usual self.\n\nDoctor: Over the past eight months, have you had days when you felt sad or down most of the day?\n\nPatient: Sad most of the day? No, I don't think so. I mean, I have days where I'm just tired, but not really sad. When I get home from work, I usually just want to put my feet up and watch something on TV. I've been watching this documentary series about the history of the postal service, which sounds boring, I know, but it's actually fascinating. Did you know they used to have these pneumatic tubes under the streets in New York City to send mail? Just these little canisters shooting around underground. It makes you think about how much infrastructure is hidden right beneath our feet, you know? Like the subway systems. I took the subway when I visited my sister in Boston last fall. She lives in Somerville, right near this amazing bakery that makes the best almond croissants. We went there every single morning. The baker, this older woman named Maria, she remembered our order by the third day. She was telling us how her family brought the recipe over from Portugal. I've always wanted to visit Europe, maybe take a train through the countryside... but um, what were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling sad. No, I haven't had days like that. Just regular days.\n\nDoctor: Over the past eight months, have you felt depressed or down more days than not?\n\nPatient: More days than not? Um, no. I don't think so. I mean, I have my moments, but definitely not more days than not. I try to stay busy, you know? Keeping busy helps. I actually started organizing my bookshelf by color last weekend. It's this whole trend I saw online, the rainbow bookshelf? It looks really striking once you get it done. But it's surprisingly hard because a lot of book spines aren't just one solid color. Like, I have this old copy of Jane Eyre, and the spine is mostly blue but with these gold letters and a little picture of a house that's sort of brownish-red. So I didn't know whether to put it with the blues or the browns. I ended up putting it with the blues next to my encyclopedias. I bought this set of encyclopedias at a garage sale years ago, before the internet was really a thing. They're bound in this beautiful navy blue leather. The woman selling them said they belonged to her grandfather who was a professor. It made me wonder what he taught. Maybe history? I love history. I was just reading about the history of the zipper the other day... but anyway, no, I haven't felt depressed more days than not.\n\nDoctor: Even if you haven’t felt depressed more days than not, have you still felt depressed or down at all during this eight-month period?\n\nPatient: Depressed at all? Um... no, not really. I mean, I guess I have moments where I feel a bit flat, but not depressed. When I feel flat, I usually try to get outside. Fresh air is so important. I was just reading this article about how indoor air quality can actually be worse than outdoor air, which is crazy when you think about all the cars and pollution. Speaking of cars, my neighbor just bought this bright yellow convertible. It's so loud when he starts it up in the morning. He works at the hospital, I think in the billing department, so he leaves really early. I always hear him leaving right when I'm making my coffee. I use a French press, which I know takes more time, but the flavor is just so much richer. I buy my beans from this local roaster who sources them ethically. He gave a whole presentation on it at the farmer's market last month. He had these pictures of the farms in Colombia... but no, I haven't been feeling depressed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It was a complete disaster, I totally missed the high notes. My friend Sarah was laughing so hard she spilled her drink on the table. It was this really sticky margarita, and we had to ask the waitress for extra napkins... but no, I haven't been feeling depressed or down like that. Just my usual self.\n\nDoctor: Over the past eight months, have you had days when you felt sad or down most of the day?\n\nPatient: Sad most of the day? No, I don't think so. I mean, I have days where I'm just tired, but not really sad. When I get home from work, I usually just want to put my feet up and watch something on TV. I've been watching this documentary series about the history of the postal service, which sounds boring, I know, but it's actually fascinating. Did you know they used to have these pneumatic tubes under the streets in New York City to send mail? Just these little canisters shooting around underground. It makes you think about how much infrastructure is hidden right beneath our feet, you know? Like the subway systems. I took the subway when I visited my sister in Boston last fall. She lives in Somerville, right near this amazing bakery that makes the best almond croissants. We went there every single morning. The baker, this older woman named Maria, she remembered our order by the third day. She was telling us how her family brought the recipe over from Portugal. I've always wanted to visit Europe, maybe take a train through the countryside... but um, what were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling sad. No, I haven't had days like that. Just regular days.\n\nDoctor: Over the past eight months, have you felt depressed or down more days than not?\n\nPatient: More days than not? Um, no. I don't think so. I mean, I have my moments, but definitely not more days than not. I try to stay busy, you know? Keeping busy helps. I actually started organizing my bookshelf by color last weekend. It's this whole trend I saw online, the rainbow bookshelf? It looks really striking once you get it done. But it's surprisingly hard because a lot of book spines aren't just one solid color. Like, I have this old copy of Jane Eyre, and the spine is mostly blue but with these gold letters and a little picture of a house that's sort of brownish-red. So I didn't know whether to put it with the blues or the browns. I ended up putting it with the blues next to my encyclopedias. I bought this set of encyclopedias at a garage sale years ago, before the internet was really a thing. They're bound in this beautiful navy blue leather. The woman selling them said they belonged to her grandfather who was a professor. It made me wonder what he taught. Maybe history? I love history. I was just reading about the history of the zipper the other day... but anyway, no, I haven't felt depressed more days than not.\n\nDoctor: Even if you haven’t felt depressed more days than not, have you still felt depressed or down at all during this eight-month period?\n\nPatient: Depressed at all? Um... no, not really. I mean, I guess I have moments where I feel a bit flat, but not depressed. When I feel flat, I usually try to get outside. Fresh air is so important. I was just reading this article about how indoor air quality can actually be worse than outdoor air, which is crazy when you think about all the cars and pollution. Speaking of cars, my neighbor just bought this bright yellow convertible. It's so loud when he starts it up in the morning. He works at the hospital, I think in the billing department, so he leaves really early. I always hear him leaving right when I'm making my coffee. I use a French press, which I know takes more time, but the flavor is just so much richer. I buy my beans from this local roaster who sources them ethically. He gave a whole presentation on it at the farmer's market last month. He had these pictures of the farms in Colombia... but no, I haven't been feeling depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any previous episodes of depression before these past eight months?\n\nPatient: Before the last eight months? I was talking to the building manager about it—Dave, his name is Dave—and he said the budget just isn't there this year because they spent it all on upgrading the HVAC system. Which, to be fair, was necessary because last summer it was just sweltering in there. I had to buy this little desk fan that plugs right into my computer. It has these soft plastic blades so if you accidentally bump it, it doesn't hurt your hand at all. I got it in this really pretty teal color. My cat actually loves it. She sits on my desk when I work from home and just stares at the fan going around and around... wait, what was the question? Oh, previous episodes of depression. No, I've never had anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Lost interest? Um... yeah, actually. That's kind of what finally brought me in. I just feel like the part of me that used to, I don't know, reach out for people or take pleasure in things has gone very quiet. Like my work, yoga, going to galleries... even journaling. It looked like I had bruised my hands or something. I was so embarrassed at work, trying to hide my hands during meetings. We have these long staff meetings on Tuesdays where they cater lunch, usually sandwiches from this deli down the street. They always forget the vegetarian options, which is frustrating for half the team. But yeah... I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. The joy is just... quiet.

I mean, I take a daily multivitamin, but I've been taking the same brand for years. I always buy it at the independent pharmacy on the corner, the one with the vintage neon sign in the window? It buzzes a little bit when it's raining outside. The pharmacist there, Brenda, she always wears these incredible hand-knit cardigans. She told me she actually sources the yarn herself from a women's cooperative in Peru, which I just think is so wonderful. Supporting fair trade is so important... in social work, you really see the impact of economic disparities firsthand, so I try to be mindful of where I shop. I’ve always wanted to visit Peru, actually, maybe hike the Inca Trail, but I read that the altitude sickness can be absolutely brutal. \n\nBut, um... no, I haven't been physically ill myself. I have this beautiful ceramic mug with little blue flowers painted on it that I use every night... but yeah, no new medicines or sickness.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any medical or neurological condition, such as thyroid disease, Parkinson’s disease, stroke, or multiple sclerosis?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I've never had any thyroid issues or anything neurological. I mean, my aunt had some thyroid trouble a few years back, and she had to completely change her diet. The owner of that store actually used to be a jazz musician, if you can believe it. He played the saxophone, and he'd sometimes just play right there in the aisles while you were browsing the vitamins. It was quite an experience, really. The conference was in Chicago, right near the lake, and the wind coming off that water in November is just piercing. I haven't been sleeping more or less than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had periods where your thinking felt noticeably slower or more foggy than usual?\n\nPatient: Foggy? No, I don't think so. My mind feels pretty clear, honestly. I actually do the crossword puzzle every morning while I have my tea, just to keep everything sharp. I use a fountain pen for it, which my coworker David says is incredibly arrogant of me. He has this very dry sense of humor... he actually used to do stand-up comedy at these little clubs down in the Village back in the nineties. He brought in a VHS tape of one of his old sets once to show us in the breakroom. It's so funny to see those old tapes, you know? It really takes you back. I remember if the VCR ate the ribbon, you had to sit there and carefully wind it all back in with a pencil. I still keep a bunch of pencils on my desk at the clinic in this little ceramic cup shaped like an owl. My niece made it for me when she was in middle school. She's just so talented with her hands, she's actually studying architecture now up in Boston. No, no slowness or fog or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of confusion or trouble knowing where you are?\n\nPatient: Confusion? No, I always know exactly where I am. I've got a pretty good sense of direction, actually. Even when I first moved to the city, I never really got lost. I used to just walk for hours on the weekends, exploring all the different neighborhoods. I found this amazing little bakery down in Little Italy once—just the most incredible cannolis you've ever had. The pastry cream was so light, not heavy and cloying like some places make it. The woman who ran it was originally from Naples, and she told me all about how her grandmother taught her the recipe. She had these beautiful black and white photographs of her family on the wall behind the counter. One of them was of her grandfather standing next to this classic vintage car... I think it was an Alfa Romeo? I've always loved those old cars, the curves on them are just like art. My dad used to restore classic cars in our garage when I was growing up. He spent three years working on a '67 Mustang. He painted it this deep, cherry red. The smell of oil and paint always reminds me of him... but anyway, no, I haven't had any trouble knowing where I am or anything like that. I'm totally oriented.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad or down. I mean, everyone has their moments, but no, not most of the day or anything like that. I actually try to keep a pretty positive outlook. I have this little daily affirmations calendar on my desk at work that really helps set the tone for the morning. It's one of those page-a-day ones, with pictures of national parks. Today's was Yosemite. I went there once, years ago, on a camping trip with some friends from grad school. We pitched our tents right in the valley, and waking up to see El Capitan in the morning light... it was just breathtaking. Though, honestly, I'm not much of a camper anymore. Sleeping on the ground really does a number on your back once you get past forty. My friend Sarah, who went on that trip with us, she actually ended up becoming a park ranger out in Colorado. He keeps a jar of milk bones right by the register... but yeah, I'm still eating fine. I mean, my life is pretty steady right now. The clinic where I work, it's a routine, you know? I've been a social worker for... gosh, going on twenty years now. I started out working with kids in the foster system, which was incredibly rewarding but definitely burns you out. I had this one supervisor back then, Brenda, she was just a force of nature. She used to wear these bright, chunky necklaces, like turquoise and coral, always clacking when she walked down the hall. She told me she bought them all on her trips to Santa Fe. I've always wanted to go to New Mexico. The light out there is supposed to be incredible for painting. I took a watercolor class at the community center a few months ago, just trying to pick up a new hobby. I tried painting this bowl of lemons I had on my kitchen counter, but getting the yellow right without making it look muddy is so much harder than it looks. The instructor, Dave, said I was using too much water on the brush. He was this very soft-spoken guy, always had paint on his cuffs... but anyway, no, nothing major or stressful has happened recently. Just the usual day-to-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energetic or 'up' and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Unusually energetic? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I usually need my solid eight hours of sleep, otherwise I'm just dragging the next day. I've always been a pretty good sleeper, actually. I bought this new mattress last year, one of those memory foam ones that comes rolled up in a box? It was so heavy getting it up the stairs to my bedroom. I had to ask my neighbor, Paul, to help me. He's such a nice guy, always tinkering with his vintage motorcycle in the driveway. It's a Triumph, I think. A really beautiful deep green color. He actually repainted it himself in his garage. He showed me the whole setup he has, with the air compressor and the spray guns. It made me think about how much patience you need for something like that, you know, getting the coats perfectly even without any drips. I don't think I'd have the steady hand for it. Just my normal routine.\n\nDoctor: During the past eight months, have there been at least two weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? For two weeks? No, I don't think I've felt like that. I mean, everyone gets a little down sometimes, but not for weeks on end. I usually try to keep my spirits up by listening to music when I'm feeling a bit blah. I have this old record player I found at a flea market a few years ago. It's one of those beautiful wooden ones from the sixties, a Zenith, I think. The speakers are built right into the cabinet. I had to get a new needle for it, which was surprisingly hard to find. I ended up ordering it from this specialty shop in Chicago. The guy I talked to on the phone, Arthur, he knew everything there is to know about vintage audio equipment. He was telling me about how he used to go to all these jazz clubs in the city back in the day. It sounded so glamorous, you know, the smoky rooms and the live bands. I've always loved jazz, especially Ella Fitzgerald. Her voice is just like velvet. I actually tried to sing one of her songs at a karaoke night once, with some friends from the clinic. It was a complete disaster, I totally missed the high notes. My friend Sarah was laughing so hard she spilled her drink on the table. It was this really sticky margarita, and we had to ask the waitress for extra napkins... but no, I haven't been feeling depressed or down like that. Just my usual self.\n\nDoctor: Over the past eight months, have you had days when you felt sad or down most of the day?\n\nPatient: Sad most of the day? No, I don't think so. I mean, I have days where I'm just tired, but not really sad. When I get home from work, I usually just want to put my feet up and watch something on TV. I've been watching this documentary series about the history of the postal service, which sounds boring, I know, but it's actually fascinating. Did you know they used to have these pneumatic tubes under the streets in New York City to send mail? Just these little canisters shooting around underground. It makes you think about how much infrastructure is hidden right beneath our feet, you know? Like the subway systems. I took the subway when I visited my sister in Boston last fall. She lives in Somerville, right near this amazing bakery that makes the best almond croissants. We went there every single morning. The baker, this older woman named Maria, she remembered our order by the third day. She was telling us how her family brought the recipe over from Portugal. I've always wanted to visit Europe, maybe take a train through the countryside... but um, what were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling sad. No, I haven't had days like that. Just regular days.\n\nDoctor: Over the past eight months, have you felt depressed or down more days than not?\n\nPatient: More days than not? Um, no. I don't think so. I mean, I have my moments, but definitely not more days than not. I try to stay busy, you know? Keeping busy helps. I actually started organizing my bookshelf by color last weekend. It's this whole trend I saw online, the rainbow bookshelf? It looks really striking once you get it done. But it's surprisingly hard because a lot of book spines aren't just one solid color. Like, I have this old copy of Jane Eyre, and the spine is mostly blue but with these gold letters and a little picture of a house that's sort of brownish-red. So I didn't know whether to put it with the blues or the browns. I ended up putting it with the blues next to my encyclopedias. I bought this set of encyclopedias at a garage sale years ago, before the internet was really a thing. They're bound in this beautiful navy blue leather. The woman selling them said they belonged to her grandfather who was a professor. It made me wonder what he taught. Maybe history? I love history. I was just reading about the history of the zipper the other day... but anyway, no, I haven't felt depressed more days than not.\n\nDoctor: Even if you haven’t felt depressed more days than not, have you still felt depressed or down at all during this eight-month period?\n\nPatient: Depressed at all? Um... no, not really. I mean, I guess I have moments where I feel a bit flat, but not depressed. When I feel flat, I usually try to get outside. Fresh air is so important. I was just reading this article about how indoor air quality can actually be worse than outdoor air, which is crazy when you think about all the cars and pollution. Speaking of cars, my neighbor just bought this bright yellow convertible. It's so loud when he starts it up in the morning. He works at the hospital, I think in the billing department, so he leaves really early. I always hear him leaving right when I'm making my coffee. I use a French press, which I know takes more time, but the flavor is just so much richer. I buy my beans from this local roaster who sources them ethically. He gave a whole presentation on it at the farmer's market last month. He had these pictures of the farms in Colombia... but no, I haven't been feeling depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any previous episodes of depression before these past eight months?\n\nPatient: Before the last eight months? No, I've never really dealt with that. I mean, I see it a lot in my line of work, obviously, being a social worker. You see all sorts of things. My office at the clinic is actually right down the hall from where they run the group sessions, so I always hear the chairs scraping on the floor. It's this awful, squeaky linoleum, you know? They really need to replace it. I was talking to the building manager about it—Dave, his name is Dave—and he said the budget just isn't there this year because they spent it all on upgrading the HVAC system. Which, to be fair, was necessary because last summer it was just sweltering in there. I had to buy this little desk fan that plugs right into my computer. It has these soft plastic blades so if you accidentally bump it, it doesn't hurt your hand at all. I got it in this really pretty teal color. My cat actually loves it. She sits on my desk when I work from home and just stares at the fan going around and around... wait, what was the question? Oh, previous episodes of depression. No, I've never had anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Even though you’ve said you don’t feel sad, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Lost interest? Um... yeah, actually. That's kind of what finally brought me in. I just feel like the part of me that used to, I don't know, reach out for people or take pleasure in things has gone very quiet. Like my work, yoga, going to galleries... even journaling. I used to journal every single morning. I have this stack of Moleskine notebooks on my nightstand. The classic black ones with the little elastic band? They're so satisfying to write in. I always use a Pilot G2 pen, the 0.38 millimeter one, because the line is so fine and it doesn't bleed through the pages. Although, I did buy a fountain pen recently. It's beautiful, a deep burgundy color with a gold nib, but it's so messy. I got ink all over my fingers the first time I tried to fill it, and it took days to wash off. It looked like I had bruised my hands or something. I was so embarrassed at work, trying to hide my hands during meetings. We have these long staff meetings on Tuesdays where they cater lunch, usually sandwiches from this deli down the street. They always forget the vegetarian options, which is frustrating for half the team. But yeah... I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. The joy is just... quiet.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It has these soft plastic blades so if you accidentally bump it, it doesn't hurt your hand at all. I got it in this really pretty teal color. My cat actually loves it. She sits on my desk when I work from home and just stares at the fan going around and around... wait, what was the question? Oh, previous episodes of depression. No, I've never had anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Even though you’ve said you don’t feel sad, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Lost interest? Um... yeah, actually. That's kind of what finally brought me in. I just feel like the part of me that used to, I don't know, reach out for people or take pleasure in things has gone very quiet. Like my work, yoga, going to galleries... even journaling. I used to journal every single morning. I have this stack of Moleskine notebooks on my nightstand. The classic black ones with the little elastic band? They're so satisfying to write in. I always use a Pilot G2 pen, the 0.38 millimeter one, because the line is so fine and it doesn't bleed through the pages. Although, I did buy a fountain pen recently. It's beautiful, a deep burgundy color with a gold nib, but it's so messy. I got ink all over my fingers the first time I tried to fill it, and it took days to wash off. It looked like I had bruised my hands or something. I was so embarrassed at work, trying to hide my hands during meetings. We have these long staff meetings on Tuesdays where they cater lunch, usually sandwiches from this deli down the street. They always forget the vegetarian options, which is frustrating for half the team. But yeah... I'm mostly just moving through obligations now. The joy is just... quiet.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt down or depressed most days for at least two years?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / mental_health_or_substance_abuse_social_worker / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Most days for two years? No, no, definitely not. Like I said, I haven't really felt depressed. Just... flat lately, I guess. Two years is a long time. My sister has been married for two years now. Her anniversary was actually last week, and they went to this incredible bed and breakfast up in the mountains. She sent me pictures of the breakfast spread they had—waffles with fresh berry compote, homemade scones, and this frittata that looked amazing. I love a good frittata, especially if you put goat cheese in it. Goat cheese makes everything better, don't you think? I buy it in those little logs from the grocery store, the ones rolled in herbs. It's so good on crackers. I usually get those water crackers, the really thin ones? They're perfectly crispy. But you have to be careful because they break so easily when you try to spread the cheese on them. It's almost an art form, honestly. I tried to serve them at a little get-together I had a few months ago, just a few friends from the clinic, and half the crackers ended up in pieces on the floor. My cat, of course, thought it was a game and started batting the crumbs under the sofa. I'm still finding pieces of cracker when I vacuum. I have one of those stick vacuums, you know, the cordless ones? It's so lightweight, which is great because my back has been bothering me a bit lately from sitting at my desk so much... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling down for two years. No, nothing like that.

Like my work, yoga, going to galleries... even journaling. I used to journal every single morning. I have this stack of Moleskine notebooks on my nightstand. She told me she actually sources the yarn herself from a women's cooperative in Peru, which I just think is so wonderful. Supporting fair trade is so important... in social work, you really see the impact of economic disparities firsthand, so I try to be mindful of where I shop. I’ve always wanted to visit Peru, actually, maybe hike the Inca Trail, but I read that the altitude sickness can be absolutely brutal. \n\nBut, um... no, I haven't been physically ill myself. And I don't use any substances or anything. I mostly just drink chamomile tea in the evenings. I have this beautiful ceramic mug with little blue flowers painted on it that I use every night... but yeah, no new medicines or sickness.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any medical or neurological condition, such as thyroid disease, Parkinson’s disease, stroke, or multiple sclerosis?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I've never had any thyroid issues or anything neurological. I mean, my aunt had some thyroid trouble a few years back, and she had to completely change her diet. She started eating all these strange seaweeds and things, which she bought from this tiny little health food store near her house. The owner of that store actually used to be a jazz musician, if you can believe it. He played the saxophone, and he'd sometimes just play right there in the aisles while you were browsing the vitamins. It was quite an experience, really. I always thought it would be wonderful to learn an instrument, maybe the cello? It just has such a mournful, beautiful sound. I saw a cellist playing in the subway once, right at the 34th Street station, and the acoustics down there just made the notes linger in the air... but no, my own health has been fine. No strokes or Parkinson's or anything like that. Just the mood changes I was telling you about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more, sleeping less, or having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Well, I haven't really noticed any big changes with my sleep. I mean, I go to bed around the same time, usually after I finish my chamomile tea. Sometimes I'll read a little bit before turning off the light. Right now I'm reading this fascinating biography of Eleanor Roosevelt. She was just such an incredible advocate, you know? The way she used her position to champion human rights... it really makes you think about the impact one person can have. It reminds me of why I got into social work in the first place. I actually met a woman at a conference once who had met Eleanor when she was a little girl. She said she had the warmest smile. The conference was in Chicago, right near the lake, and the wind coming off that water in November is just piercing. I had to buy a completely new scarf while I was there, this lovely thick wool one, plum colored. I still wear it when it gets really cold here. But um, yeah, my sleep has been pretty normal, I think. I haven't been sleeping more or less than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had periods where your thinking felt noticeably slower or more foggy than usual?\n\nPatient: Foggy? No, I don't think so. My mind feels pretty clear, honestly. I actually do the crossword puzzle every morning while I have my tea, just to keep everything sharp. I use a fountain pen for it, which my coworker David says is incredibly arrogant of me. He has this very dry sense of humor... he actually used to do stand-up comedy at these little clubs down in the Village back in the nineties. He brought in a VHS tape of one of his old sets once to show us in the breakroom. It's so funny to see those old tapes, you know? It really takes you back. I remember if the VCR ate the ribbon, you had to sit there and carefully wind it all back in with a pencil. I still keep a bunch of pencils on my desk at the clinic in this little ceramic cup shaped like an owl. My niece made it for me when she was in middle school. She's just so talented with her hands, she's actually studying architecture now up in Boston. The winters there are just brutal, though, much worse than here, with all that historic brick just freezing through. But... wait, I'm sorry, what were you asking? Oh, right, my thinking. No, no slowness or fog or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of confusion or trouble knowing where you are?\n\nPatient: Confusion? No, I always know exactly where I am. I've got a pretty good sense of direction, actually. Even when I first moved to the city, I never really got lost. I used to just walk for hours on the weekends, exploring all the different neighborhoods. I found this amazing little bakery down in Little Italy once—just the most incredible cannolis you've ever had. The pastry cream was so light, not heavy and cloying like some places make it. The woman who ran it was originally from Naples, and she told me all about how her grandmother taught her the recipe. She had these beautiful black and white photographs of her family on the wall behind the counter. One of them was of her grandfather standing next to this classic vintage car... I think it was an Alfa Romeo? I've always loved those old cars, the curves on them are just like art. My dad used to restore classic cars in our garage when I was growing up. He spent three years working on a '67 Mustang. He painted it this deep, cherry red. The smell of oil and paint always reminds me of him... but anyway, no, I haven't had any trouble knowing where I am or anything like that. I'm totally oriented.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad or down. I mean, everyone has their moments, but no, not most of the day or anything like that. I actually try to keep a pretty positive outlook. I have this little daily affirmations calendar on my desk at work that really helps set the tone for the morning. It's one of those page-a-day ones, with pictures of national parks. Today's was Yosemite. I went there once, years ago, on a camping trip with some friends from grad school. We pitched our tents right in the valley, and waking up to see El Capitan in the morning light... it was just breathtaking. Though, honestly, I'm not much of a camper anymore. Sleeping on the ground really does a number on your back once you get past forty. My friend Sarah, who went on that trip with us, she actually ended up becoming a park ranger out in Colorado. She sends me a postcard every Christmas with a different mountain peak on it. I have them all pinned up on a corkboard in my kitchen. The corkboard is framed in this really pretty reclaimed barn wood that I found at a flea market upstate... but um, yeah, I'm not feeling sad. My mood has been fine, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost your appetite?\n\nPatient: Appetite? No, not really. I mean, I'm still eating my normal meals. I usually make a turkey sandwich for lunch to take to the clinic. I like to use that thick-cut whole wheat bread, the kind with all the seeds in it? It's so much better than that flimsy white bread that just turns to mush the second you put a slice of tomato on it. I actually grew my own tomatoes last summer in these big terracotta pots on my balcony. They were those little cherry tomatoes, the Sun Gold ones, so sweet you could just eat them like candy. I had this one pot that just went crazy, vines spilling everywhere. I had to tie them up to the railing with some twine. I buy my twine from this little hardware store down the street, it's one of those great old places where the floors creak and they have every size of screw sorted into these tiny little wooden drawers. The guy who owns it, Mr. Henderson, he's been there since the seventies. He always gives my dog a treat when we walk by. He keeps a jar of milk bones right by the register... but yeah, I'm still eating fine. My appetite hasn't changed.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms begin after a stressful life event or major adjustment in your life?\n\nPatient: Stressful event? No, I don't think so. I mean, my life is pretty steady right now. The clinic where I work, it's a routine, you know? I've been a social worker for... gosh, going on twenty years now. I started out working with kids in the foster system, which was incredibly rewarding but definitely burns you out. I had this one supervisor back then, Brenda, she was just a force of nature. She used to wear these bright, chunky necklaces, like turquoise and coral, always clacking when she walked down the hall. She told me she bought them all on her trips to Santa Fe. I've always wanted to go to New Mexico. The light out there is supposed to be incredible for painting. I took a watercolor class at the community center a few months ago, just trying to pick up a new hobby. I tried painting this bowl of lemons I had on my kitchen counter, but getting the yellow right without making it look muddy is so much harder than it looks. The instructor, Dave, said I was using too much water on the brush. He was this very soft-spoken guy, always had paint on his cuffs... but anyway, no, nothing major or stressful has happened recently. Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I usually need my solid eight hours of sleep, otherwise I'm just dragging the next day. I've always been a pretty good sleeper, actually. I bought this new mattress last year, one of those memory foam ones that comes rolled up in a box? It was so heavy getting it up the stairs to my bedroom. I had to ask my neighbor, Paul, to help me. He's such a nice guy, always tinkering with his vintage motorcycle in the driveway. It's a Triumph, I think. A really beautiful deep green color. He actually repainted it himself in his garage. He showed me the whole setup he has, with the air compressor and the spray guns. It made me think about how much patience you need for something like that, you know, getting the coats perfectly even without any drips. I don't think I'd have the steady hand for it. I tried to frost a cake for my niece's birthday last month—she turned seven, wanted a mermaid theme—and the icing was just a lumpy disaster. I tried to smooth it out with a warm spatula, which is a trick I saw on a baking show, but it just made the colors bleed together into this weird grayish-blue... but um, no, I haven't had any periods where I felt super energetic or didn't need sleep. Just my normal routine.\n\nDoctor: During the past eight months, have there been at least two weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? For two weeks? No, I don't think I've felt like that. I mean, everyone gets a little down sometimes, but not for weeks on end. I usually try to keep my spirits up by listening to music when I'm feeling a bit blah. I have this old record player I found at a flea market a few years ago. It's one of those beautiful wooden ones from the sixties, a Zenith, I think. The speakers are built right into the cabinet. I had to get a new needle for it, which was surprisingly hard to find. I ended up ordering it from this specialty shop in Chicago. The guy I talked to on the phone, Arthur, he knew everything there is to know about vintage audio equipment. He was telling me about how he used to go to all these jazz clubs in the city back in the day. It sounded so glamorous, you know, the smoky rooms and the live bands. I've always loved jazz, especially Ella Fitzgerald. Her voice is just like velvet. I actually tried to sing one of her songs at a karaoke night once, with some friends from the clinic. Just my usual self.\n\nDoctor: Over the past eight months, have you had days when you felt sad or down most of the day?\n\nPatient: Sad most of the day? No, I don't think so. Like, I have this old copy of Jane Eyre, and the spine is mostly blue but with these gold letters and a little picture of a house that's sort of brownish-red. Um... no, not really. I mean, I guess I have moments where I feel a bit flat, but not depressed. When I feel flat, I usually try to get outside. Fresh air is so important. I was just reading this article about how indoor air quality can actually be worse than outdoor air, which is crazy when you think about all the cars and pollution. Speaking of cars, my neighbor just bought this bright yellow convertible. It's so loud when he starts it up in the morning. He works at the hospital, I think in the billing department, so he leaves really early. I always hear him leaving right when I'm making my coffee. I use a French press, which I know takes more time, but the flavor is just so much richer. I buy my beans from this local roaster who sources them ethically. He gave a whole presentation on it at the farmer's market last month. She sits on my desk when I work from home and just stares at the fan going around and around... wait, what was the question? Oh, previous episodes of depression. Like my work, yoga, going to galleries... even journaling. I used to journal every single morning. I have this stack of Moleskine notebooks on my nightstand. The classic black ones with the little elastic band? They're so satisfying to write in. I always use a Pilot G2 pen, the 0.38 millimeter one, because the line is so fine and it doesn't bleed through the pages. Although, I did buy a fountain pen recently. It's beautiful, a deep burgundy color with a gold nib, but it's so messy. I got ink all over my fingers the first time I tried to fill it, and it took days to wash off. It looked like I had bruised my hands or something. I was so embarrassed at work, trying to hide my hands during meetings.
